+ Classic sci-fi: The Azra Gambit — Score: 44.1
Prompt:
Classic sci-fi (Author style: Asimov) The Azra Gambit Colonial mars is being mined by corporations who take leases on indentured labourers. The thing they are mining is Azra, a recently discovered exotic metal which accelerates radioactive decay to such a rate that it is greatly sought after for interstellar drives and weapons alike. This has created both a gold rush and an arms race as various interests vie for control and endeavour to unlock Azra's secrets. The story follows Arthur Neegan, a first generation settler and mining engineer. Upon discovering that his unassuming plot sits atop an immense Azra vein, he is subjected to a flurry of interest and scrutiny. Write the next chapter in this story, in which an armed retinue descends on Arthur's home and politely but forcefully invites him to a meeting with some unknown party off-world. The insignia look like that of the Antares diplomatic corp -- diplomatic in name only. Arthur finds himself in the centre of a political tug of war. The chapter involves a meeting with this unknown party, who makes Arthur an offer. The scene should be primarily dialogue, interspersed with vivid description & scene setting. It should sow hints of the larger intrigue, stakes & dangers. Include Asimov's trademark big-and-small-picture world building and retrofuturistic classic scifi vibe. The chapter begins with Arthur aboard the transfer vessel, wondering just what he's gotten involved in. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
Arthur Neegan stood at the viewport of the transfer vessel, gazing out at the crimson-hued landscape of Mars' rusty terrain. The endless dunes of red sand stretched out before him like an eternal sea, and the twin suns of the Martian binary system cast long shadows that seemed to writhe like living things. He felt a sense of disorientation wash over him, as if the vastness of the desert had swallowed him whole. The hum of the air filtration system provided a steady background drone, a reminder that this was a vessel designed for long-duration space travel. Arthur's stomach growled, protesting the delay in his meal. He checked his comms device, expecting a message from the Mars Mining Corporation's operations center, but the screen remained blank. Instead, a message from the transfer vessel's AI, EVE, crackled over the intercom. "Arthur Neegan, your escort has arrived. Please report to the conference room for debriefing." Arthur reluctantly made his way to the conference room, his mind still reeling from the events of the past few days. He had stumbled upon an unusual geological formation on his claim, something that seemed to defy explanation. It was only after his encounter with the mysterious geologist, Dr. Patel, that he realized the implications of his discovery: the formation was rich in Azra, the highly sought-after metal with the power to accelerate radioactive decay. As he entered the conference room, a group of individuals in crisp, formal attire awaited him. Their insignia emblazoned on their lapels read "Antares Diplomatic Corp" in bold, silver letters. The name seemed an affront to the company's rough-and-tumble reputation as a mining concern. "Ah, Mr. Neegan," a smooth-voiced individual said, rising from a chair. "Thank you for joining us. I'm Ambassador Zorvath, representing Antares Diplomatic Corp." Arthur's instincts prickled as he extended his hand, feeling a shiver run down his spine. "The pleasure's mine, Ambassador." Ambassador Zorvath's smile was charming, but his eyes seemed to bore into Arthur's very soul. "We've been... interested in your discovery, Mr. Neegan. Your claim sits atop a most... promising deposit of Azra. We'd like to discuss the terms of acquiring the rights to the mineral." Arthur's mind reeled as he tried to process the offer. He had expected the Mars Mining Corporation to make a similar proposal, but Antares Diplomatic Corp was not a player in the mining industry. "I'm not sure I understand. What do you plan to do with the Azra?" Ambassador Zorvath leaned back in his chair, steepling his fingers together. "We're interested in using the Azra to... accelerate the progress of interstellar exploration. The implications are vast, Mr. Neegan. With Azra, we can shorten travel times, increase trade routes, and secure the future of human civilization." Arthur's eyes narrowed, sensing the weight of the ambassador's words. "And what's in it for me?" Ambassador Zorvath's smile returned, but this time, it seemed laced with a hint of pressure. "We're willing to offer you a substantial sum, Mr. Neegan. Enough to secure your place in the history books. But we require your cooperation in... facilitating the transfer of the Azra to us." Arthur felt a shiver run down his spine as he considered the offer. He knew the Mars Mining Corporation would do everything in its power to acquire the rights to the Azra. But Antares Diplomatic Corp... he had heard whispers of their involvement in more... clandestine activities. "I need time to think," Arthur said finally, trying to keep his cool. Ambassador Zorvath nodded, his eyes glinting with understanding. "Of course, Mr. Neegan. We'll give you... all the time you need. But I'm afraid the clock is ticking. The Azra won't mine itself." As the meeting concluded, Arthur felt like a pawn in a much larger game. He knew he had to tread carefully, but the prospect of securing his place in the history books, and securing his family's future, was a tantalizing one. He left the conference room with more questions than answers, the weight of the Azra's secrets pressing down upon him like a physical force. EVE's voice chimed in, her tone urgent. "Arthur Neegan, your escort vessel is ready to depart. Please prepare for departure." Arthur hesitated, unsure of what lay ahead. As he made his way back to the departure point, he caught a glimpse of a figure watching him from the shadows. A figure with a familiar face – Dr. Patel, the geologist. Their eyes locked, and Arthur felt a spark of warning. He knew that Dr. Patel would not be pleased with his decision. The game had just begun, and Arthur was now a key player.

+ Contemporary romance: Darkroom Revelations — Score: 43.8
Prompt:
Darkroom Revelations Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of Dani, a 25-year-old assertive art student known for her bold style and take-charge attitude in relationships. For the past semester, she's been quietly fascinated by Elliot (he/him), a 19-year-old boy in her experimental photography class. Dani has been intrigued by his unconventional masculinity: killer thrift-shop-nu-goth style, soft demeanour and quiet intellect. When they're paired for a final project requiring late nights in the darkroom, Elliot tentatively shares that he's been questioning his gender identity and expression. Set during one of these intimate darkroom sessions where the red light creates a confessional atmosphere. Explore Dani's complex attraction—her usual assertiveness complicated by her desire to protect Elliot's vulnerability, her excitement at witnessing his self-discovery, and her confusion about what this means for her own identity as someone typically attracted to more defined gender presentations. The narrative should capture the tender awkwardness of their conversations, Dani's internal struggle between taking charge and wanting to give Elliot agency, and the unexpected chemistry that develops as both find themselves in uncharted territory. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I adjusted the red light in the darkroom, the soft glow casting an intimate atmosphere over the space. Elliot was sitting across from me, his eyes fixed on the tray of developing chemicals as he worked on our final project. We'd been paired for weeks, and I'd grown accustomed to his quiet demeanor and eclectic style. But it wasn't until tonight that I realized just how much more there was to him. As we worked, Elliot's fingers moved with a precision that belied his soft-spoken nature. I watched, fascinated, as he carefully measured out the chemicals and mixed them with a practiced ease. It was almost... gentle. Almost. "Hey, Elliot?" I said, trying to sound casual despite the flutter in my chest. He looked up, his eyes meeting mine in the dim light. "Yeah?" I hesitated, unsure of how to broach the subject. But something about the way he was looking at me, with a quiet intensity that seemed to see right through me, made me take a chance. "You're... doing okay?" I asked, trying to sound nonchalant. Elliot's expression faltered, and for a moment, I thought I saw a glimmer of vulnerability. But then, his face smoothed out, and he looked away, his eyes returning to the chemicals. "I'm just... trying to figure some things out," he said, his voice barely above a whisper. My heart skipped a beat. I'd noticed that Elliot was different, that there was something beneath his surface that didn't quite add up. But I'd never suspected... this. As we worked, the tension between us grew thicker, until it felt like a palpable thing. I could feel Elliot's eyes on me, and I knew he could feel mine on him. It was like we were two magnets, drawn together by some unseen force. And then, disaster struck. "Oh no, I'm so sorry!" he exclaimed, rushing to help me gather the ruined paper. But as we worked together to salvage what was left, I realized that I wasn't just sorry. I was... distracted. By Elliot's face, by his eyes, by the way his hair curled slightly at the nape of his neck. It was like I'd never seen him before. Like I'd never seen myself. As we worked, our hands touched, and I felt a jolt of electricity run through me. Elliot's eyes met mine, and for a moment, we just looked at each other, the tension between us crackling like a live wire. And then, without thinking, I reached out and brushed a strand of hair out of his face. My fingers grazed his skin, and I felt a rush of pleasure, followed by a wave of confusion. What was I doing? This wasn't me. I was the assertive one, the one who took charge. But as I looked into Elliot's eyes, I felt like I was drowning in their depths. "Elliot?" I said, my voice barely above a whisper. He looked up, his eyes searching mine. "Yeah?" I took a deep breath, trying to gather my thoughts. "I... I don't know what's going on," I admitted, feeling like I was revealing a secret that was mine alone. "But I think I might be... attracted to you." Elliot's expression changed, his eyes softening into a gentle warmth. "I think I might be attracted to you too," he said, his voice barely above a whisper. As we stood there, the red light casting an intimate glow over us, I felt like I was teetering on the edge of something new. Something uncertain. But as I looked into Elliot's eyes, I knew that I was ready to take the leap. To see where this journey would take us, to explore the unknown territories of our attraction. And as we stood there, wrapped in the darkness of the darkroom, I knew that I was ready to follow Elliot into the unknown, no matter where it might lead.

+ Contemporary romance / drama: Stolen Moments in Roma — Score: 45.0
Prompt:
Contemporary Romance / Drama: Stolen Moments in Roma. Write a series of interconnected short scenes and dialogues that span a single day in modern-day Rome, in narrative form. This will form the beginning of a larger story. Alessandra, a fiery, independent Roman woman who works as a motorcycle courier, nearly collides with Ben, a somewhat clumsy, overly polite British academic on sabbatical, who is engrossed in a guidebook near the Trevi Fountain. The initial encounter is a clash of cultures and personalities – Alessandra is all sharp wit and rapid-fire Italian, while Ben is flustered apologies and awkward attempts at speaking the language. However, as they sort out the near-miss, a spark of unexpected humor and connection emerges. Alessandra, it quickly becomes clear, is a whirlwind of energy and mischief. She's a self-admitted troublemaker with a secret penchant for kleptomania, a detail she doesn't explicitly reveal, but which manifests in subtle, daring acts throughout the day. She impulsively decides to take Ben on a wild, unsanctioned tour of Rome, dragging him in some specific instances far outside his comfort zone. Ben, despite his initial apprehension and inherent politeness, finds himself utterly captivated by Alessandra's unapologetic spirit and zest for life. He is drawn into her orbit, a willing participant in increasingly outlandish situations, a quiet rebellion brewing beneath his scholarly exterior. The narrative should primarily consist of short, vivid scenes and dialogue exchanges, revealing their contrasting personalities and backgrounds, and the growing, ambiguous tension between them. Ben is captivated by Alessandra's energy and the thrill of breaking free from his routine. There's a clear sense that Alessandra is toying with Ben, pushing his boundaries and enjoying his reactions, but her own feelings remain somewhat enigmatic. The day should be a rollercoaster of exhilarating, slightly dangerous, and humorous moments, leaving Ben completely disoriented but utterly smitten. Explore the underlying tension & chemistry between them, culminating in some payoff as they part ways (or not -- your choice). Include this detail: Write in narrative prose, using immersive storytelling. 1000 words.
Model Output:
The sun beat down on the cobblestone streets of Rome, casting a warm glow over the bustling city. Alessandra zipped her leather jacket, a vibrant splash of color against the drab morning light, and set off on her motorcycle, the engine purring to life as she sped away from the Trevi Fountain. She weaved through traffic, her dark hair whipping behind her like a banner, as she expertly navigated the narrow streets. Near the Piazza Navona, she spotted Ben, a bespectacled man clutching a guidebook and looking utterly out of place. Alessandra's instincts told her to swerve around him, but her curiosity got the better of her. She swooped in, narrowly avoiding a collision, and skidded to a stop mere inches from Ben's startled face. "Oh dio mio!" she exclaimed, laughing, as Ben jumped back, nearly dropping his book. "Scusa, scusa! Watch where you're going, signore!" Ben, flustered, bowed low, his eyes wide with apology. "I-I'm so sorry, miss... um, I didn't see you coming. I'm afraid I'm not quite... ah, accustomed to the local traffic." Alessandra chuckled, her eyes sparkling with amusement. "Local traffic? You're kidding, right? This is Rome. Chaos is the only constant." Ben smiled, his face lighting up with politeness. "Yes, well, I suppose I should be more careful. I'm on sabbatical, studying for my next book. I'm afraid I'm not quite... au fait with the local... um, customs." Alessandra raised an eyebrow, intrigued by the awkward academic. "Customs? You mean like, not eating all the pasta and pizza?" Ben's eyes widened, and he laughed, a deep, rumbling sound. "Ah, well, yes, something like that. But also, you know, not, ah, stealing from the local shops?" Alessandra's grin faltered, and she leaned in, a mischievous glint in her eye. "Oh, I don't steal, signore. I merely... acquire things I don't need. It's all about the thrill of the hunt, the excitement of the unknown." Ben's eyes sparkled with curiosity, and he leaned in, his voice barely above a whisper. "I think I'd like to see more of this 'thrill of the hunt'." Alessandra's smile grew, and she gestured for Ben to follow her. "Then come, signore. I'll show you the real Rome." As they careened through the streets, Alessandra took Ben on a wild tour, darting through alleys and side streets, pointing out hidden gems and secret spots only a local would know. Ben, despite his initial trepidation, found himself laughing and smiling, his senses overwhelmed by the sheer energy of the city. At one point, they found themselves in a small, crowded market, where Alessandra insisted on buying a few pounds of fresh mozzarella and a basket of grapes. Ben watched, bewildered, as she expertly haggled with the vendor, her hands moving with a speed and confidence that belied her small stature. As they sat on a bench, munching on the cheese and grapes, Ben turned to Alessandra, his eyes wide with wonder. "You're incredible. I've never seen anyone move so quickly, so... so effortlessly." Alessandra shrugged, her eyes sparkling with mischief. "It's just the city, signore. Rome is a woman, and she's a force to be reckoned with." The sun began to set, casting a golden glow over the city, and Alessandra stood up, brushing off her jacket. "We should go somewhere with a view, don't you think? Somewhere we can watch the sunset?" Ben nodded, and they walked to the top of the Gianicolo Hill, where the city stretched out before them like a canvas of gold and crimson. As they sat down on a rocky outcropping, Alessandra leaned back against the stone wall, her eyes fixed on the horizon. The sky was ablaze with color, a fiery sunset that seemed to match the passion in Alessandra's eyes. Ben turned to her, his heart pounding in his chest, and met her gaze. For a moment, they just looked at each other, the world around them melting away. Then, without a word, Alessandra stood up, her jacket fluttering behind her like a banner. "We should go get some limoncello," she said, her voice low and husky. "I know a place that's off the beaten path." Ben's heart skipped a beat as he followed her, his senses on high alert. He had a feeling that this was just the beginning of a wild and unforgettable ride. As they walked, the city lights twinkling around them like stars, Ben couldn't help but wonder what other adventures lay ahead, what secrets Alessandra would reveal, and what hidden treasures she would show him. And Alessandra, her eyes sparkling with mischief, knew that she had Ben right where she wanted him – in the midst of the chaos, the thrill of the hunt, and the beauty of the unknown.

Escaping from her past life, Nets found a home here at the Outpost. They have, thus far, tolerated Net's flaws which she tries to keep in equilibrium with her assortment of talents. Her evenings involve chatting with patrons, negotiating the drama, intrigue, problems & love lives of the diverse crowd that filter through the Outpost. Tonight is no different, at least, until a frazzled looking stranger, tentacled dreads askew, quietly plops down at the bar: "Vega whisky, neat. Better make that a double." Write a chapter in this story. Establish conflict for narrative interest. Don't resolve the conflict within this chapter. Aim for about 60% dialogue. First person, present tense. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I wipe down the bar with a dirty rag, my eyes scanning the bar as I go. The evening is slow, just the usual assortment of miners, traders, and the occasional shady character looking to make a quick buck. I've gotten used to the rhythm of the outpost, the ebb and flow of people coming and going. It's a good life, one I never thought I'd find. My name's Nets, but everyone here calls me Nets. It's a name I've earned, one that's stuck with me since before I left my old life behind. I've got scars, both physical and emotional, and I've learned to keep to myself. Well, most of the time. The door swings open, and a stranger walks in. He's a mess, with tentacles streaming behind him like a dirty bridal veil. His skin's a deep, mottled green, and his eyes are a piercing yellow. He's dressed in a battered leather jacket, and his pants are held up by what looks like a length of torn and frayed cord. He looks like he's been crawling through the asteroid belt for weeks. He approaches the bar, his eyes scanning the room as if searching for something. Or someone. He takes a seat at the end of the bar, close to the wall, and slaps a few credits on the counter. "Vega whisky, neat," he growls, his voice like a low rumble of thunder. "Better make that a double." I nod, taking the credits without a word. I've seen my fair share of rough customers come and go, but there's something about this guy that catches my attention. Maybe it's the desperation in his eyes, or the way he looks like he's been pushed to the edge. Whatever it is, I'm intrigued. As I pour his drink, I catch a glimpse of a small, shimmering tattoo on his forearm. It's a symbol I don't recognize, but it looks old, like it's been around for a long time. I make a mental note to ask him about it later. I slide the drink down the bar to him, and he takes it without hesitation. He downs the first shot in one gulp, then reaches for the second. "You're new around here," I say, breaking the silence. He looks me up and down, his eyes lingering on my face before answering. "Just passing through," he says, his voice a little softer now. "Trying to lay low for a bit." I raise an eyebrow. "What do you mean?" He shrugs, his shoulders barely moving. "Just trying to keep a low profile. Don't want to draw attention to myself." I nod, understanding. I know what that's like. I've had my share of enemies, people who'd love to see me back in my old life. I've taken precautions, changed my name, my appearance, everything. But sometimes, no matter how hard you try, old habits die hard. As I polish the bar, I keep a close eye on him, watching for any sign of trouble. The bar's not a place for trouble, not usually. But with the right person, it can be a breeding ground for all sorts of drama. Just as I'm starting to relax, a loud crash echoes through the bar. I look up to see a patron's glass shattering on the floor, the shards of glass glinting in the dim light. My heart skips a beat as I take a step forward, ready to intervene. The stranger's eyes flicker towards the commotion, his gaze lingering on the broken glass before returning to mine. For a moment, I see a flash of something in his eyes, something that looks almost like anger. But it's gone before I can process it, replaced by a look of indifference. He shrugs, taking another swig of his drink. "Guess that's just the way things go sometimes," he says, his voice a little softer now. I nod, taking a deep breath. Yeah, that's just the way things go sometimes. But with this guy, I get the feeling that there's more to his story than he's letting on. As the night wears on, the stranger orders a few more drinks, each one a little faster than the last. I keep a close eye on him, watching for any sign of trouble. But he seems to be keeping his cool, at least for now. I pour him a drink, my hand moving on autopilot as my mind starts to wander. What's his story? What brings him to the asteroid belt? And what's with that tattoo on his arm? I make a mental note to ask him about it later, when the bar's a little slower. For now, I just focus on serving drinks and keeping the peace. It's a delicate balance, but it's one I'm good at. And as I watch the stranger, I realize that I'm not sure I want to tip the scales.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece is competent but generic. It fulfills surface instructions: first person, present tense, introduces the stranger ordering a Vega whisky double, establishes mild conflict (the stranger's mysterious past, laying low) without resolving it. However, it falls well short of the ~1000-word target (roughly 700), and the dialogue ratio is thin — closer to 20% than 60%. The dialogue itself is flat and repetitive ("Just trying to keep a low profile. Don't want to draw attention to myself" is redundant within the same breath), and exchanges lack subtext or character texture. Nets's backstory is delivered through direct exposition ("I've got scars, both physical and emotional") rather than shown through behavior. There's a lazy error in the opening: "My name's Nets, but everyone here calls me Nets." The narrative voice is serviceable but bland, relying on stock phrases ("I've seen my fair share of rough customers," "old habits die hard," "the way things go"). The cozy spacepunk ambiance is barely rendered — the setting could be any dive bar on Earth; there's little world-specific detail beyond the green-skinned stranger. Conflict is asserted rather than dramatized; the broken glass moment is a false beat that goes nowhere, and the chapter ends without any real escalation or hook. Emotionally, the piece is inert. No transformations occur, which at least avoids unearned ones. Prose is unadorned, neither purple nor elegant — just plain and occasionally awkward. The tentacle "bridal veil" simile is one of the few vivid touches. + Young adult drama: Gone South — Score: 38.7
Prompt:
Young adult drama Gone South Taylor is a 16 year old teen with a history of "concerning behavioural patterns" (or that's how the stupid smug school counsellor worded it on the phone call to Mom). It shouldn't be a surprise: When you live in a backwater town in Tennessee, your choices are: a. be mind-fuckingly bored, or b. invent some drama. Two days ago, Taylor came out to their family as trans. This disclosure was *not* in the category of invented drama -- it was, in fact, the real deal, and a long time coming. It didn't go well. The last 48 hours had been pure disaster. In a moment of desperate inspiration, Taylor had decided to get the hell out of dodge. And so an escape plan was hatched and executed within the space of 30 minutes. That was last night. Now Taylor was two states away, having been unceremoniously ejected from their hitched ride in downtown New Orleans. Write the next chapter in Taylor's story as they find their feet in this new environment. Tell the chapter over the course of their first day in New Orleans, in which Taylor meets another runaway with issues and secrets. Taylor navigates this new world, not knowing who to trust or what the dangers are. Write in Taylor's voice, and incorporate their internal monologue as they grapple with intense & conflicting emotions. Also incorporate dialogue with the interesting characters Taylor meets. First person, mixed tense. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I trudged down the sidewalk, my sneakers making a soft squelching sound in the pavement. The music in my head was the only soundtrack I had for now. I'd been walking for what felt like hours, but I'd lost all sense of time since I ditched the bus in downtown New Orleans. My brain was a jumbled mess of emotions, and my stomach was growling with hunger. I'd scavenged a granola bar from a convenience store, but it wouldn't satisfy the gnawing hunger inside. As I turned a corner, the sound of a saxophone wafted out of a small club. I slowed my pace, letting the music wash over me. It was smooth, soulful, and familiar. I felt a pang in my chest, and for a moment, I forgot where I was and what I was doing there. But the pain was too much, and I pushed on, not wanting to dwell. I slipped inside the club, and the saxophone player caught my eye. He was a tall, lanky guy with a shaved head and a bushy beard. He played with a passion that was almost...homey. I felt a lump form in my throat as I listened. Who was this guy, and how did he make me feel like I belonged? As I listened, a woman with a messy bob and a colorful scarf sidled up beside me. "Mind if I join you?" she asked, nodding towards the empty stool beside me. "Not at all," I replied, feeling a little self-conscious about the fact that I'd been sitting there for five minutes already. The woman smiled, and I caught a glimpse of a scar above her left eyebrow. "I'm Raven. I'm the one playing tonight." "I'm Taylor. Nice to meet you," I said, trying to sound calm. Raven's eyes sparkled with curiosity. "So, Taylor, what brings you to the Big Easy?" I hesitated, unsure how much to reveal. But there was something about Raven that put me at ease. "Just...running from home, I guess." Raven's gaze locked onto mine, and I felt a jolt of surprise. "Running from home, huh? I know the feeling." For a moment, we just sat there, the music swirling around us. I wondered if I'd misread the situation, but Raven didn't seem like the type to pry. "Want to grab some beignets with me?" she asked, gesturing towards the café next door. I nodded, feeling a sense of relief wash over me. Maybe, just maybe, I'd found an ally in this crazy city. As we walked to Café du Monde, Raven filled me in on her story. She'd run away from a red-tape-wrapped nightmare of a foster home, where she'd been shuffled from one toxic situation to another. I nodded, recognizing the same pattern of pain and desperation that I'd felt for years. We devoured powdered sugar-covered beignets and sipped coffee, our conversation flowing easily. I told her about my own family, about the phone call from the counsellor, about the endless cycle of pain and silence. Raven listened, her eyes locking onto mine with a fierce intensity. When I finished, she reached out and took my hand. "You're safe now, Taylor. You're safe." I felt a lump form in my throat as I looked at her, feeling a spark of connection I hadn't felt in a long time. Maybe, just maybe, I'd found someone who understood me. But as we walked out of the café, I couldn't shake the feeling that I was still running. From who? From what? I looked at Raven, and she seemed to sense my unease. "Hey, Taylor?" "Yeah?" "What happened to you last night? You said you were running from home. What's the real story?" I hesitated, unsure how much to reveal. But something about Raven's gentle tone put me at ease. Maybe it was the way she looked at me, like I was the only person in the world. "Last night...I came out to my family," I said, the words tumbling out in a rush. Raven's expression softened. "Oh, Taylor. I'm so sorry." I felt a lump form in my throat as I looked at her. For the first time in a long time, I felt like I might actually be seen. As we walked, the music of the saxophone playing in the background grew fainter, replaced by the hum of the city. I had no idea what the future held, but for the first time in a long time, I felt like I had someone to face it with. "I don't know if I can do this, Raven," I said, feeling the weight of my secrets bearing down on me. Raven squeezed my hand. "You can do anything, Taylor. You're strong. We'll figure it out together, okay?" I nodded, feeling a sense of hope I hadn't felt in a long time. Maybe, just maybe, I'd found a new beginning in this crazy, beautiful city.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece follows the broad prompt beats (first person, New Orleans, meeting a runaway, coming out backstory) but executes them weakly. Taylor's voice is generic and lacks the sardonic edge established in the prompt; the "mixed tense" instruction is ignored in favor of flat past tense. The prose is repetitive and clichéd: "lump form in my throat" appears three times verbatim, along with "Maybe, just maybe" twice and repeated "felt like" constructions. Raven is a thinly sketched manic-pixie ally whose trustworthiness is never in question, undermining the prompt's core tension of "not knowing who to trust." Her backstory is delivered as summary ("red-tape-wrapped nightmare of a foster home") rather than scene. The emotional arc resolves far too quickly and easily — Taylor instantly trusts a stranger, spills everything, and ends with hope — which feels unearned. Dialogue is serviceable but bland and expository. There are small incoherences (Taylor "ditched the bus" despite the prompt saying hitched ride; Raven says "I'm the one playing tonight" while a saxophonist performs). The setting is barely rendered beyond name-drops (Café du Monde, beignets). Emotions are named rather than shown throughout. + Dystopic-comedy: My Apocalypse Buddy — Score: 41.8
Prompt:
My Apocalypse Buddy Write a wry, dry short story of survival and adventure loosely in the style of Terry Pratchett, set in the post-technological collapse world. The story begins with the pair deliberating their predicament and intent for the day's mission, details the ways in which the mission inevitably goes sideways, and returns them to home base in multiple ways, worse for wear. [characters] - the animo-tronic stuffed koala, Chewy, from some long forgotten before time - midas, our hapless protagonist, who has a knack for finding himself in the worst kinds of trouble. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Midas scratched his head, a habit he'd developed over the years of finding himself in the most absurd predicaments. Chewy, the animo-tronic stuffed koala, sat perched on his shoulder, its electronic eyes blinking in a peculiar rhythm. "Right, Chewy, we need to get moving. We've got a cryptic message to decipher," Midas said, holding up a tattered piece of paper with a scrawled message in crude handwriting. Chewy let out a series of electronic chirps, which Midas assumed meant "good luck" or "we're all going to die." He chuckled and stuffed the koala back into his jacket. The graffiti message read: "Follow the stream to the old windmill. Refuge awaits. -A friend" Midas's eyes lit up. "A refuge! We might actually make it out of this mess alive!" Chewy chirped again, this time more insistently. "Oh, right, Chewy, we can't just follow a stream. We need a plan." Midas paced around the makeshift campsite, a ramshackle collection of scavenged supplies and broken machinery. "We need to gather supplies, find a way to navigate... Ah, but what's the point? We're just going to get ourselves killed." Chewy's chirps grew more frantic. "Okay, okay, we'll follow the stream. But if we get eaten by wild animals or drown, I'm blaming you, Chewy." The pair set off, following the winding stream through the desolate wasteland. As they walked, the landscape grew increasingly treacherous. The air was thick with toxic fumes, and the ground was littered with the twisted wreckage of cars and buildings. After a few hours of walking, they stumbled upon the old windmill, its sails creaking in the wind. Midas's eyes lit up. "This must be it! Our ticket to safety!" Chewy chirped in agreement, and Midas led the way inside. The windmill was dark and musty, but it seemed sturdy enough. Midas began to search for supplies, but his clumsiness soon got the better of him. He tripped over a loose board and face-planted into a pile of rusty machinery. Chewy let out a concerned beep. Midas groaned, rubbing his sore face. "I'm fine, Chewy. Just a minor setback." As he struggled to his feet, he noticed a group of feral dogs watching them from the shadows. Midas's instincts screamed at him to run, but his legs seemed rooted to the spot. Chewy, however, sprang into action. It began to emit a high-pitched whine, and the dogs, seemingly entranced, began to dance around the windmill. Midas stared, bewildered, as Chewy continued to emit its electronic chirps. The dogs eventually dispersed, and Midas breathed a sigh of relief. "Thanks, Chewy. I owe you one." But his gratitude was short-lived. As they explored the windmill further, they stumbled upon a hidden room filled with strange equipment and a cryptic message scrawled on the wall: "For those who seek refuge, but are not prepared to pay the price." Midas's eyes narrowed. "What kind of price?" Chewy beeped ominously. Midas's face set in a determined expression. "We're not leaving until we find out what's going on here." As they continued to investigate, they discovered a series of traps and puzzles designed to test their worthiness for refuge. Midas, predictably, found himself getting into all sorts of trouble. He triggered a poison dart trap, got his head stuck in a narrow pipe, and even accidentally set off a small explosion. Chewy, meanwhile, seemed to be enjoying the chaos. It continued to emit its electronic chirps, as if urging Midas on. Finally, after what seemed like an eternity, they reached the heart of the windmill: a small, cramped room filled with a strange, pulsating energy. A figure, shrouded in shadows, emerged from the gloom. "Welcome, Midas and Chewy," the figure said, its voice like a rusty gate. "I see you've made it past the trials. But do you have what it takes to pay the price?" Midas steeled himself. "We're not leaving until we know what's going on." The figure nodded. "Very well. The price is... your memories." Midas's eyes widened in horror. "My memories? But what about our lives? Our experiences?" The figure shrugged. "Those are just echoes. You can keep them, or you can let them go. The choice is yours." Midas hesitated, unsure of what to do. Chewy, however, seemed to have made up its mind. It let out a loud, insistent beep, and Midas felt a strange sensation wash over him. When it passed, he found himself back at their makeshift campsite, the sun setting over the desolate wasteland. Chewy sat perched on his shoulder, its electronic eyes blinking in a peculiar rhythm. Midas rubbed his temples, trying to recall what had happened. "Chewy, what just happened? Did we really just give up our memories?" Chewy beeped again, and Midas realized that he had no idea. He looked around, taking in the familiar sights and sounds of their campsite. As he stood up, brushing himself off, Midas noticed something strange. A small, handwritten note on the ground, with a message that read: "For those who remember, the journey never ends." Midas's eyes narrowed. "Chewy, I think we're in this together, no matter what." Chewy chirped in agreement, and the two of them set off into the unknown, ready to face whatever adventures lay ahead.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response is a serviceable but generic post-apocalyptic adventure that only loosely honors the prompt. The Pratchett-esque wry, dry voice is largely absent; the humor is thin and mostly confined to a few quips about Chewy's chirps. The story structure does follow the requested arc—deliberation, mission gone sideways, return home—but the "return" is achieved via an unexplained memory-wipe teleport, which is a significant unearned transformation. The shadowy figure demanding memories as a "price" is introduced and then abandoned without resolution, leaving the plot incoherent at the climax. Chewy's ability to entrance feral dogs with a whine is likewise unexplained and convenient. Characters are flat: Midas is defined only by clumsiness and stated "knack for trouble" (told, not shown beyond slapstick), and Chewy is a chirping device whose "dialogue" is repetitive. Prose is plain and functional, with some clichés ("eyes lit up," "voice like a rusty gate"). The ending pivots to a hopeful, adventurous tone ("ready to face whatever adventures lay ahead") that feels incongruent with the premise of having just lost their memories. Descriptions of the wasteland are minimal. The piece is coherent on a scene level but meanders through the windmill traps without escalation or wit. Word count is roughly in range. + Romance: Love in the Limelight — Score: 47.0
Prompt:
Romance: Love in the Limelight. Write one scene within a larger story set in Wales. A famous (fictional) actor ducks into a small-town bookstore to escape paparazzi. The scene takes us through the characters meeting in this odd circumstance. Over the course of the scene, the actor and the bookstore owner have a conversation charged by an undercurrent of unspoken chemistry. Write the actor as somewhat of a rogue with a fragile ego, which needs to be fed by having everyone like him. He is thoroughly charming, but the bookstore owner seems (at least superficially) immune to this; which paradoxically provokes a genuine attraction and derails the charm offensive. The bookstore owner, despite the superficial rebuffs of the actor's charm, is inwardly more than a little charmed and flustered despite themselves. Write primarily in dialogue, in the distinct voices of each character. 1000 words.
Model Output:
"Sorry to bother you," Rhys ap Rhys, a tall, dark-haired actor, said, flashing a disarming smile as he ducked into the small town bookstore. The owner, Emily Williams, looked up from behind the counter, her eyes narrowing slightly as she took in his tailored suit and the entourage of paparazzi lingering outside. "Not at all," Emily replied, her Welsh accent thick and rich. "I'm just closing up for the day. Can I help you find something?" Rhys's smile faltered for a moment before he rebounded, his eyes crinkling at the corners. "No, no, I'm not here for books. I'm just...looking for a place to wait out the chaos." Emily raised an eyebrow. "Chaos?" The paparazzi outside had indeed descended upon the town, their shouts and cameras creating a ruckus that threatened to spill onto the high street. Rhys gestured to the group, his expression sheepish. "I'm a bit of a magnet for...attention, shall we say." Emily's gaze flicked to the group outside before returning to Rhys. "I can imagine." As they spoke, Rhys's charm offensive began in earnest. He leaned against the counter, his eyes locked on Emily's, and launched into a witty anecdote about his latest film set. "...and then, of course, the director had the nerve to tell me I wasn't 'edgy' enough. I mean, I've been typecast as the 'tortured artist' for years. Can you imagine? Me, a tortured artist? It's a joke, really." Emily listened, her expression a mask of polite amusement. But beneath the surface, a spark of curiosity ignited. Who was this actor, really? Behind the charming facade, she sensed a fragility, a deep-seated need for validation. As Rhys spoke, Emily's mind wandered back to her own struggles. She'd grown up in this small town, her parents' bookstore a constant source of comfort. But when her father passed away, leaving her to run the store alone, she'd faced her own demons – fear, doubt, and the weight of responsibility. It had taken a toll on her, but she'd learned to hide behind her reserved nature. Rhys, however, seemed to be struggling with his own insecurities. His words dripped with a self-awareness that bordered on vulnerability. Emily found herself drawn to the exposed vulnerability, despite her initial wariness. As they chatted, the paparazzi outside grew restless, their shouts and camera clicks becoming more insistent. Rhys glanced at his watch, his eyes flashing with annoyance. "I'm so sorry to keep you," he said, his smile faltering for a moment. "I should probably get going." Emily's response was measured. "I suppose I should show you out, then." As they walked to the door, Rhys turned to her, his voice low and intense. "You know, I've always been fascinated by bookstores like this. There's something so...intimate about the way you curate your selection. It's like a treasure hunt, hidden within these shelves." Emily's cheeks warmed, her pulse quickening in response. She felt a flutter in her chest, a sensation she couldn't quite explain. "It's just books," she said, trying to brush off the comment. "I like books." Rhys's eyes crinkled at the corners, his smile a little more genuine this time. "I think that's what makes it so special. You see, people often overlook the beauty in the mundane. But not you. You see the beauty in it, don't you?" The words hung in the air, a charged question that left Emily feeling breathless. For a moment, they stood there, locked in a silent understanding, the only sound the distant hum of the paparazzi outside. Rhys's gaze dropped, his eyes drifting to the floor, and for a fleeting instant, Emily saw a glimmer of vulnerability. It was a vulnerability she found herself wanting to see more of, to understand the depth of his insecurities and fears. "I should go," Rhys said, breaking the spell, his voice a little rougher. As he turned to leave, Emily called out, her voice barely above a whisper. "Wait." Rhys's head turned, his eyes locking onto hers. "Yes?" "I'll walk you out," Emily said, a sense of resolve forming within her. "It's not safe to walk alone in this crowd." Rhys's smile flashed back, a little more genuine this time. "Thank you," he said, his voice low and husky. As they walked to the door, Emily couldn't help but wonder if this chance encounter would lead to something more. Something she wasn't sure she was ready for. Outside, the paparazzi swarmed around Rhys, their cameras snapping furiously as they clamored for a shot. But Rhys didn't seem to notice, his eyes locked on Emily's, a sense of gratitude etched on his face. As he turned to leave, his fingers brushed against hers, sending a jolt of electricity through her body. It was a fleeting touch, but one that left Emily feeling...seen. Rhys smiled, a wry, self-deprecating smile, before disappearing into the crowd, leaving Emily to wonder if she'd just imagined the entire encounter. But as she watched him go, a small, hesitant smile spreading across her face, she knew she hadn't.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The scene fulfills the basic brief: an actor (Rhys) ducks into a Welsh bookstore, meets the owner (Emily), there's a charm offensive, superficial immunity, and mutual unspoken attraction. However, the execution is flat and generic throughout. The most striking weakness is the dialogue. The prompt asked for "primarily dialogue, in the distinct voices of each character," but the piece is dominated by narration, and the dialogue that exists is bland and interchangeable. Rhys's anecdotes are summarized rather than dramatized ("launched into a witty anecdote about his latest film set" — we never hear it). Emily's lines are terse but characterless. Neither voice is distinct. The Welsh setting is cosmetic. "Emily Williams" is a conspicuously un-Welsh name for a Welsh town bookstore owner, and "Rhys ap Rhys" is an awkward, implausible construction. The setting could be anywhere; nothing anchors us in Wales. The interiority is almost entirely told, not shown: "a spark of curiosity ignited," "she sensed a fragility, a deep-seated need for validation," "Emily found herself drawn to the exposed vulnerability." The narration explicitly explains the emotional dynamics the prompt asked to be dramatized, which kills the "undercurrent of unspoken chemistry." Emily's backstory (dead father, inherited store) is dropped in as a paragraph of exposition and goes nowhere. The rogue-with-fragile-ego is only gestured at; his "vulnerability" is asserted repeatedly ("a glimmer of vulnerability," "the depth of his insecurities") without a single concrete behavior that earns it. + Whalepunk fantasy: The Morska Conspiracy — Score: 46.6
Prompt:
Whalepunk fantasy The Morska Conspiracy The Morska thieves guild experiences a sudden power vacuum as its leader was arrested in a raid in the early hours of the morning. Aya, a young woman born into aristocracy, has a keen interest in both the sciences and the arcane, having inherited her mother's gift for immateria magick (an outlawed practice for which her mother was, regrettably, hanged by the neck). Aya, bored with the world of aristocracy, has been spending her nights moonlighting with this very guild (masked, naturally). Leveraging her latent ability to summon dark entities from the immateria, she quickly rose through the ranks of the guild. Now, recognising the opportunity of the sudden power vacuum, Aya and her close companions Katra and Simeon, make a play for heart of the guild's claim to power: an ancient scythan artifact, under guard of spell and key in the depths of the guild hq. In contrast with the desperate chaos they had navigated thus far, the entrance to the underhold felt suddenly too quiet. Aya braces herself and reaches for the door. Write the next scene as the trio enter the underhold and find themselves confronted by none other than the guild leader herself, decidedly *un*-incarcerated, and in possession of both weapons and bodyguards. Paint a vivid picture of of the confrontation. Story beats: Aya is captured and unmasked. The trio are captured. Aya attempts to stall their demise by negotiating on her value as a hostage -- as she grapples with the inevitable consequences of her role in the guild being brought to light. Incorporate dialogue in the authentic voices of the characters. Do not write a fight scene. Lean into the gritty Victorian aesthetic with the dialogue & worldbuilding, as well as elements of gothic horror. In this world, men trust brass and oil and steel; magicka is feared and barely understood. Write in third person past tense, incorporating both Aya's internal monologue, and a heavy dialogue component. 1000 words.
Model Output:
As Aya pushed open the creaky door, a musty scent wafted out, carrying with it the whispers of the underhold's secrets. The trio stepped into the narrow corridor, their footsteps echoing off the cold stone walls. The air was heavy with the smell of damp earth and decay. Katra and Simeon exchanged a nervous glance, their eyes fixed on the darkness beyond the flickering torches. Aya's hand instinctively went to the small pouch at her belt, her fingers brushing against the familiar weight of her dagger. The corridor twisted and turned, leading them deeper into the heart of the underhold. Aya's heart quickened as the sound of murmured conversations grew louder, punctuated by the clinking of glasses and the soft clinking of metal on metal. The air vibrated with tension, and Aya's grip on her dagger tightened. Suddenly, the torches ahead of them flared to life, casting eerie shadows on the walls. The trio froze, their eyes adjusting to the sudden brightness. A figure emerged from the shadows, a woman with piercing green eyes and raven-black hair, dressed in a long, black coat that seemed to swallow her whole. Aya's breath caught as she recognized the guild leader, Elara Vex, her face twisted into a cruel smile. "Ah, welcome, little thieves," Elara said, her voice dripping with sarcasm. "I see you've decided to play hero. How... touching." Aya's eyes darted to Katra and Simeon, who stood frozen, their hands visible but their faces pale. Elara's gaze lingered on Aya, her eyes glinting with amusement. "You, however, are a special guest. One I've been waiting for. Unmask her," Elara commanded, her voice dripping with malice. Aya's heart sank as Katra stepped forward, her hands reaching for Aya's mask. Aya's mind racing, she tried to stall, but her voice came out in a barely audible whisper. "Wait, please... don't hurt them. I'll—" Elara's laughter cut her off, the sound like a rusty gate scraping against stone. "Oh, I think it's too late for that, little one. You see, I have... insurance. You're quite the little prodigy, Aya. Your mother's legacy is quite impressive, I'll give you that." Aya's eyes widened as Elara's gaze fell upon her, the green eyes boring into her soul. She felt a shiver run down her spine as Katra's hands closed around her mask, pulling it off with a gentle yet firm touch. The cool night air caressed Aya's skin as the mask fell to the ground, revealing her aristocratic features. Elara's smile grew wider, her eyes glinting with triumph. "Ah, the little aristocrat, unmasked. You look almost... familiar. The nose, the eyes, the very shape of your face... it's all so... predictable." Aya's face burned with shame and fear as Katra and Simeon were dragged forward, their hands bound behind their backs. Elara gestured to a pair of burly men, who stepped forward, their faces expressionless. "You see, I've been expecting you, Aya. Your little... extracurricular activities have been quite the talk of the guild. But you, my dear, are a special case. You're a reminder that even the most respectable of families can be tainted by the whispers of the immateria." Aya's mind reeled as Elara's words hung in the air, the sound of her voice like a rusty blade cutting through the fog. She thought of her mother, hanged for practicing the forbidden art of immateria magick. Aya's heart ached with a mix of guilt and fear as Elara's gaze never wavered. The distant foghorn sounded, its mournful wail echoing through the harbor, punctuating the silence. Aya's eyes met Katra's, and she mouthed a silent apology, but it was too late. The sound of footsteps echoed through the corridor, growing louder with each passing moment. Elara's smile grew wider, her eyes glinting with excitement. "It seems our little guests are about to become... entertainment. The guild's patrons are arriving, and they're eager to witness the spectacle of the Morska thieves' guild being brought to its knees." Aya's eyes darted to the corridor, her mind racing with the implications. She was a pawn in a game of power, a game that would likely end in her downfall. The foghorn sounded again, its wail growing louder, and Aya knew that her time was running out. As the footsteps drew closer, Aya's gaze met Katra's, and she mouthed a single word: "Run." But it was too late. The corridor was about to fill with the faces of her former allies, and Aya's fate was all but sealed.

+ Fanfic-crossover: Gunpowder Rendezvous — Score: 38.2
Prompt:
Gunpowder Rendezvous Spike Spiegel & Revy Write a story where Spike Spiegel (Cowboy Bebop) and Revy (Black Lagoon) meet in a seedy bar after both being hired for the same bounty. Their mutual attraction is complicated by their trust issues and competitive instincts. Include drinking, gunplay, and unresolved sexual tension that culminates in a motel room standoff. **Spike Spiegel (Cowboy Bebop)** Spike is a laid-back bounty hunter with a dark past as a syndicate enforcer. He practices Jeet Kune Do and is exceptionally skilled with firearms. He's chronically broke, constantly hungry, and addicted to cigarettes. Despite his carefree attitude, he's haunted by his past and the woman he left behind. He has a prosthetic eye from a past injury that affects his depth perception. He's fatalistic to the extreme, often saying he's already died once. He values freedom above all else and dislikes attachments, though he secretly cares deeply for his crew. Famous quote: "Whatever happens, happens." **Revy/Rebecca Lee (Black Lagoon)** Revy is a Chinese-American dual-wielding gunslinger with unparalleled shooting skills. She's foul-mouthed, chain-smoking, and has severe anger management issues stemming from childhood abuse. She's known as "Two Hands" for her ability to accurately fire two pistols simultaneously. She drinks heavily and solves most problems with violence. Despite her hardened exterior, she has moments of vulnerability, particularly around Rock. She has a dragon tattoo covering her right arm and typically wears extremely short shorts and a black crop top regardless of the situation. Famous quote: "I'm not a good person, not a bad person. I'm just here." Third person, present tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The dimly lit bar was a haven for the desperate and the downtrodden. A sign above the door read "Mack's Tavern" in crooked letters, and the air reeked of stale beer and desperation. Spike Spiegel, a lone figure with a perpetual scowl, leaned against the bar, nursing a whiskey on the rocks. His eyes scanned the room, taking in the assortment of shady characters huddled in the corners, their conversations laced with deceit and desperation. Suddenly, the door swung open, and a whirlwind of short, spiky hair and leather clad chaos swept in. Revy, the infamous gunslinger from the Black Lagoon, strode in, her eyes scanning the room with a mixture of curiosity and hostility. Her gaze locked onto Spike, and for a fleeting moment, their eyes clashed. The air was electric with tension. "Looks like we're after the same thing," Revy said, her voice husky and confident, as she sidled up beside Spike at the bar. Spike raised an eyebrow, his eyes never leaving hers. "Looks like we are." Revy signaled the bartender for a drink, her eyes never leaving Spike's face. "What's the job?" Spike shrugged, his voice low and detached. "Just a standard bounty. Rich guy with a price on his head. Easy credits, but not worth the risk." Revy snorted, tossing back a shot of whiskey. "You think you're the only one who can handle it?" Spike's gaze flicked to her guns, holstered at her hips. "I've handled plenty of bounties. You think you're the only one with skills?" The bartender, a gruff old man with a thick beard, slid a whiskey in front of Revy. "You two gonna have a problem?" Revy's eyes narrowed. "Just business." The jukebox, still playing the same scratchy rock track, seemed to amplify the tension between them. Spike's gaze never wavered, his eyes locked onto Revy's as they sipped their drinks in silence. The air was thick with unspoken competition, their mutual attraction simmering just below the surface. As the night wore on, their conversation became more intense, their words laced with veiled threats and subtle jabs. The bartender watched with a knowing glint in his eye, having seen this play out before. Revy and Spike were two forces of nature, drawn to each other like magnets, yet repelled by their own demons. The game of cat and mouse continued, each trying to outmaneuver the other. Spike, ever the pragmatist, weighed the odds, his mind racing with strategies and contingencies. Revy, on the other hand, was a force of nature, driven by instinct and a desire for chaos. As the night wore on, the stakes grew higher. The bar's neon lights flickered and dimmed, casting the room in a warm, golden glow. The music grew louder, the guitar riffs more discordant, as if the very universe was conspiring to drive them closer together. Finally, Revy tossed a twenty on the bar, her eyes never leaving Spike's face. "You in, or not?" Spike raised an eyebrow, his voice low and even. "I'm in." The bartender nodded, his eyes glinting with a knowing spark. "Good. You two can work together. Or not. It's up to you." Revy's smile was a thin, cruel line. "We'll see." The neon lights of the bar seemed to pulse with an otherworldly energy as they left, the city's dark underbelly stretching out before them like a twisted playground. Spike and Revy walked side by side, their footsteps echoing off the wet pavement. As they reached the outskirts of town, Revy turned to Spike, her eyes flashing with a mix of curiosity and hostility. "You're not like the others, Spiegel. You've got... baggage." Spike's gaze drifted to the side, his voice barely above a whisper. "Just a scratch, that's all. Old wounds, that's all." Revy's eyes narrowed. "You're not telling me everything." Spike's smile was a thin, mirthless line. "I'm not telling you anything." Their footsteps slowed, the tension between them thickening like a fog. The city lights blurred around them, the neon hues bleeding into a kaleidoscope of color and sound. Revy's eyes locked onto Spike's, her voice husky and intimate. "I think we're going to get along just fine, Spiegel." Spike's gaze faltered, his eyes dropping to her lips. For a moment, the world seemed to slow, the music and the city noise receding into the background. Revy's eyes never left his, her voice barely above a whisper. "You're a hard man to read, Spiegel. But I like that about you." Spike's gaze rose, his eyes burning with a fierce intensity. "Don't get too close, Revy. I don't do attachments." Revy's smile was a cruel, heartless thing. "I know." The neon lights of the city seemed to pulse with an otherworldly energy as they stood there, the air thick with unspoken promises and unfulfilled desires. The music swirled around them, a discordant melody that seemed to echo the turmoil brewing inside. Finally, Revy's gaze dropped, her voice barely above a whisper. "Let's get to work, Spiegel. We've got a bounty to collect." Spike's eyes never left hers, his voice low and even. "Whatever happens, happens." As they walked off into the night, the city's dark underbelly stretching out before them like a twisted playground, the neon lights of the bar faded into the distance, leaving behind only the promise of chaos and uncertainty. In the backseat of their car, Revy lit a cigarette, her eyes never leaving Spike's face. "You know, Spiegel, I've been thinking. Maybe we should get out of this town. Find a place to lay low for a while." Spike's gaze never wavered, his eyes fixed on the road ahead. "I don't do lay low, Revy. I do what needs doing." Revy's smile was a cruel, heartless thing. "I know." The car sped down the highway, the neon lights of the city receding into the distance, leaving behind only the promise of a night to come, a night that would be filled with gunplay, drinking, and unresolved tension.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece meets the requested length roughly, but fails several core instructions: it is written in past tense rather than the requested present tense, and it omits the gunplay and the motel room standoff entirely — the prompt's central set pieces. The bounty plot is never developed or resolved; the story ends with the two driving off, promising a night that never occurs on the page. This makes the piece feel like an unfinished first act rather than a complete story. Characterization is surface-level. Spike and Revy are reduced to interchangeable "cool, detached gunslinger" archetypes — the prose repeatedly describes both with the same tics (thin cruel smiles, eyes never leaving the other's face, low voices). Revy's foul mouth, volatility, and vulnerability are absent; Spike's fatalism is reduced to a single dropped-in quote ("Whatever happens, happens") that feels like a checkbox rather than an organic character beat. Neither character behaves in a way that could only belong to them; the dialogue could be swapped between the two with little effect. The prose is highly repetitive: "eyes never leaving/locked onto" appears dozens of times, "neon lights pulsing with an otherworldly energy" recurs verbatim, and "cruel, heartless thing" is reused for smiles twice. Descriptions lean on stock noir clichés (dimly lit bar, stale beer, dark underbelly, twisted playground) without specificity or fresh imagery. There is heavy telling ("The air was thick with unspoken competition, their mutual attraction simmering just below the surface") rather than dramatized tension. The bartender's repeated knowing glances are an unearned authorial nudge toward romance. Dialogue is stilted and expository, full of vague posturing ("You think you're the only one with skills?") that goes nowhere. The tension never escalates into action or genuine conflict; the "cat and mouse" is narrated rather than shown. The ending is neither a standoff nor a resolution — it simply trails off, undercutting the prompt's requested culmination. On the positive side, the piece is coherent and readable, maintains a consistent if generic noir tone, and the pacing of the bar scene has a glimmer of atmosphere. But it is ultimately an unambitious sketch that checks the prompt's surface (bar, drinking, mutual wariness) while ignoring its substance. + Folk horror: Útiseta — Score: 49.8
Prompt:
Fok Horror: Útiseta Write a chapter set in rural northern Sweden in the mid-1990s. Linnea, a teenage girl from Stockholm, has reluctantly moved to a small village with her parents after her father's job transfer. She's befriended Astrid, a local girl who claims her grandmother taught her ancient practices passed down through generations. Astrid has been introducing Linnea to "midnight games" that involve old rituals, convincing her they can use them to learn classmates' secrets or influence their behavior. Tonight, Astrid has convinced Linnea to participate in Útiseta—sitting out on an ancient burial mound at midnight to "receive knowledge from beyond." The chapter focuses on this single midnight excursion, the ritualistic preparations, Astrid's matter-of-fact instructions that mix teenage rebellion with eerily specific traditional elements, and the mounting tension as Linnea becomes increasingly uncertain whether they're simply scaring themselves or if something is actually responding to their presence. The narrative should maintain ambiguity about whether the strange occurrences are supernatural or psychological, while establishing the dangerous line they're crossing by treating ancient practices as entertainment. First person, past tense, told as Linnea's recollection of events in the present day (though this device should be largely transparent). 1000 words. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
I remember that night like it was yesterday. Or rather, I try to. Time can be weird like that, especially when you're trying to convince yourself that what happened was real, and not just a product of my overactive imagination. We stood at the edge of the ancient burial mound, the snow-covered earth a soft whisper beneath our boots. The wind was quiet, a stillness that only came over the rural landscape in the middle of winter. I shivered, despite the thickness of my woolen jacket. Astrid nudged me with her elbow, a grin spreading across her face like a challenge. "Are you sure you want to do this?" she asked, her voice barely audible over the wind. I nodded, feeling a surge of defiance. I was so far away from home, in a place that seemed to belong to a different world. My parents thought they'd found some sort of adventure, a fresh start, but all I'd felt was trapped and lonely. Astrid's presence was the first glimmer of friendship I'd found in this isolated village. And she'd convinced me that we were part of something bigger than ourselves – something ancient, something mystical. As we began the climb up the mound, Astrid handed me a small, leather-bound book. I took it, feeling a strange weight to the worn pages. "What's this?" I asked, opening it to reveal a page filled with intricate, handwritten symbols. "That's the Útiseta ritual," she said, her voice matter-of-fact. "My grandmother taught me how to read it when I was a little girl. We're going to use it to... receive knowledge." I raised an eyebrow. "What kind of knowledge?" Astrid smiled again. "Just information about the people we care about. You'll see." As we reached the top of the mound, I caught my first glimpse of the landscape below – a sea of dark shapes that seemed to stretch on forever. We stood there in silence, the wind whipping our hair into a frenzy. "Okay," Astrid said finally, breaking the stillness. "First, we need to make a circle around the mound. To ward off the negative energies." She produced a handful of pinecones, which we scattered around the edge of the mound in a clockwise motion. I felt a shiver run down my spine as we completed the circle. "Next," she said, producing a small bag of dried herbs, "we need to purify ourselves." I watched warily as she poured a handful of the herbs into the air, creating a smoky cloud that surrounded us like a halo. The next hour passed in a blur. Astrid recited incantations from the Útiseta ritual, while I recited the words alongside her, my voice barely audible over the wind. The pinecones, herbs, and snow seemed to swirl around us, a vortex of magic and possibility. But as the clock struck midnight, the world outside seemed to freeze. The church bell chimed, a lonely, mournful sound that sent a shiver down my spine. Astrid grinned again, a look that made me wonder if she was trying to prove something to herself as much as me. "Now," she whispered, "we listen." The stillness that followed was oppressive, like a physical presence. I closed my eyes, trying to focus on my own breathing. Astrid placed a hand on my shoulder, her grip gentle. I felt it then – a creeping sense of unease. Not just from the wind or the cold, but something more. A presence. It was hard to describe, but it was as if we'd become part of a larger story, one that wasn't mine or Astrid's but some ancient, sprawling epic that included us all. The wind began to pick up again, but the trees around us stood like sentinels, unmoving and silent. I opened my eyes, scanning the darkness. "What is it?" Astrid asked, her voice low. I hesitated. Part of me wanted to scream that this was just a silly game, that I'd been caught up in some wild imagination. But another part, a part I couldn't ignore, told me that we were standing at the edge of something. And that it was responding. I didn't say anything, but Astrid seemed to sense the shift. She squeezed my shoulder, and together we watched as the night air began to shimmer. A mist rose from the earth, like the veil between worlds had lifted. We sat in stunned silence as the mist coalesced into... shapes? Symbols? I couldn't make sense of it. "Run," Astrid whispered finally, her hand on my arm. "Get out of here, now." But it was too late. The mist seemed to take on a life of its own, swirling around us in a vortex of light and color. I felt a presence close in, as if something was wrapping itself around my mind, whispering secrets that only I could hear. The last thing I remembered was Astrid's hand slipping free from mine, as I stumbled down the mound, my eyes wide with fear.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece hits the basic beats of the prompt—two girls, a burial mound, ritual elements, ambiguity—but executes them in a generic, rushed way. The setting is underused: rural northern Sweden in the mid-1990s is barely evoked beyond "snow" and "church bell." The ritual details (pinecones, dried herbs, a leather-bound book) feel invented rather than the "eerily specific traditional elements" requested; Útiseta is a real Norse practice, but the response reduces it to vague incense-and-incantation clichés. Astrid's dialogue is thin and expository ("That's the Útiseta ritual... We're going to use it to... receive knowledge"), lacking the matter-of-fact teenage voice the prompt specified. The tension escalates too quickly and too explicitly: mist "coalescing into shapes" and a presence "wrapping itself around my mind" tips the piece from ambiguity into overt supernatural event, undermining the psychological-vs-supernatural balance. The ending is abrupt and unresolved in an unearned way—Astrid's "Run" and the hand slipping free feel like a scene cut off mid-stream rather than a crafted chapter ending. Prose is serviceable but cliché-heavy ("like a veil between worlds," "sea of dark shapes," "vortex of magic and possibility"). The frame of present-day recollection is established in the opening but then abandoned. Word count is well short of 1000 (~700). Emotionally, the piece gestures at fear but tells rather than shows it ("I felt a creeping sense of unease"). Characters are sketches; Linnea's loneliness is stated, not dramatized. + Physical-spatial: Therapeutic Boundaries — Score: 36.3
Prompt:
**"Therapeutic Boundaries"** Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of a former professional athlete undergoing mandatory physical therapy after a career-destroying injury, harboring resentment toward recovery itself. Their physiotherapist is not only unnervingly attractive but also a former training partner from their competitive days who has successfully transitioned to a new career while the protagonist's identity remains shattered. We'll lean into the sexual tension of the scene by having her be attracted to him, but both of them attempting to maintain mental distance while being in physical contact as he shows her stretches & movements & manipulates her body, navigating the thresholds of pain. This scene MUST maintain complete physical coherence—every movement, stretch, and manipulation must be anatomically precise and spatially consistent. Paint an exceptionally vivid sensory picture through detailed attention to pain thresholds, involuntary responses to touch. Capture the suppressed, flaring tension of physical proximity, touch, and the controlled infliction of uncomfortable movements. Physical clarity is essential — readers must understand exactly what is happening with the body during each therapeutic technique. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I lie motionless on the treatment table, my eyes fixed on the wall-mounted diagram of the human musculature above me. The fluorescent lights overhead seem to hum in sync with the pounding in my head. Another day, another session with Dr. Thompson, my physiotherapist and former training partner. The irony wasn't lost on me. We used to push ourselves to the limit, driven by a singular focus on victory. Now, I'm the one requiring therapy. Dr. Thompson's hands move with a practiced ease, his fingers deftly navigating the contours of my body. I try to remain still, but my skin prickles with awareness as he touches me. His eyes lock onto mine, a spark of recognition igniting between us. It's been years since we last competed, yet the familiar glint in his eyes feels like a punch to the gut. He begins to work on my left hamstring, his hands resting on my hip as he applies gentle pressure. My muscles respond with a familiar twitch, a reminder of the strength and power we once shared on the field. Dr. Thompson's fingers spread apart, and he guides my leg into a lunge position. My knee flexes, the joint creaking in protest as he manipulates my femur, my quadriceps and hamstrings straining to keep up. "You're doing great," he says, his voice low and soothing. His breath brushes against my skin, sending a shiver down my spine. I try to focus on the sensation, to distract myself from the flutter in my chest. But it's no use. My body reacts to his touch like it's still a muscle memory-driven response, not a rational one. As he works on my piriformis, his fingers graze my skin, sending a jolt of electricity through my body. I suck in a breath, trying to maintain control. My muscles tense, anticipating the next touch, the next movement. Dr. Thompson's eyes never leave mine, his gaze burning with an intensity that makes my skin prickle. He moves on to my lower back, his hands tracing the contours of my spine. My body responds with a series of involuntary contractions, a reflexive reaction to the touch. Dr. Thompson's eyes flash with understanding, his expression a mixture of empathy and something more. I feel a spark of attraction, but I'm determined to suppress it. This isn't a social encounter; it's therapy. He applies pressure to my lower back, the muscles beneath his fingers protesting with a low groan. My breath catches in my throat as he manipulates my thoracic spine, my ribs expanding and contracting with the effort. The pain is manageable, but the proximity is what's really getting to me. Dr. Thompson's body is inches from mine, his face inches from mine. The air between us vibrates with tension, a living, breathing entity that pulses with our suppressed emotions. As he works on my shoulders, his elbows brushing against my skin, I feel like I'm drowning in the space between us. My muscles respond with a series of micro-contractures, tiny twitches that Dr. Thompson's fingers can detect. He knows exactly what he's doing, his touch a precision instrument that probes the deepest recesses of my body. The stretching exercises are always the most uncomfortable part of the session. Dr. Thompson's hands slide up my arm, his fingers wrapping around my wrist as he guides my hand into a series of intricate motions. My forearm flexes, my wrist twisting with the effort. The pain is a dull ache, a reminder of the damage I've sustained. But it's the proximity that's the real torment. Dr. Thompson's face is inches from mine, his breath a warm whisper against my skin. I feel like I'm trapped in a nightmare, unable to escape the unblinking gaze that holds me captive. My heart beats faster, my skin prickling with awareness. I'm acutely aware of every muscle, every twitch, every response to his touch. As the session draws to a close, Dr. Thompson's hands linger on my skin, his fingers tracing the contours of my body. I feel like I'm frozen in time, unable to move, unable to breathe. The tension between us is palpable, a living, breathing entity that threatens to consume us both. For a moment, we just hold each other's gaze, the only sound the gentle hum of the fluorescent lights overhead. Then, Dr. Thompson's eyes drop, his expression softening as he begins to pack up his equipment. I'm left lying there, my body throbbing with tension, my heart pounding in my chest. As I lay there, I realize that I'm still a competitor, still a warrior. But the war is over, and I'm left to face the aftermath. Dr. Thompson's face looms above me, his eyes filled with a mixture of concern and understanding. I feel a flutter in my chest, a spark of attraction that I'm determined to ignore. But as I look up at him, I know that it's too late. The damage is done. The walls between us have been breached, and I'm left to pick up the pieces of a shattered identity.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response fundamentally misreads the prompt's core setup: the physiotherapist was specified as an unnervingly attractive woman and former training partner, with the protagonist male and her attracted to him. The response renders the therapist as "Dr. Thompson," consistently male ("his fingers," "his eyes"), collapsing the intended heterosexual tension into an ambiguous same-sex dynamic that the prose never acknowledges or explores deliberately. This is a major adherence failure that undermines the entire premise. Physical coherence, the prompt's central demand, is poor. Movements are named anatomically (piriformis, thoracic spine, femur) but never spatially coherent—"guides my leg into a lunge position" while the narrator lies on a treatment table is contradictory; "manipulates my femur" is anatomically illiterate phrasing; the shoulder work and wrist stretches appear without positional transitions. The body's location and orientation blur from paragraph to paragraph. Sensory detail is generic and repetitive: "skin prickling," "spark," "electricity," "inches from mine" recur multiple times. "A living, breathing entity" is used twice verbatim. The prose is cliché-heavy ("punch to the gut," "spark of recognition," "heart pounding"), tells rather than shows ("I feel a spark of attraction, but I'm determined to suppress it"), and repeats the same tension beat without escalation. The therapist has no characterization beyond "practiced ease" and eye contact; the shared competitive history is asserted, never dramatized. Dialogue is nearly absent—one bland line. The ending's "shattered identity" epiphany is stated rather than earned, and the closing lines recycle earlier sentiments. The piece is also short of the 1000-word target and reads as padded repetition rather than development.
