+ First-person unique perspective: Overthinking It — Score: 50.2
Prompt:
Overthinking It Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of an intelligent 8th-grade girl with social anxiety who has developed a crush on a classmate. The narrative takes place during a group science project where they're paired together, as she cycles through rehearsed conversations while second-guessing each potential approach. Her inner thoughts should reveal the gap between her articulate internal voice and her difficulty expressing herself aloud, her fears of coming across as desperate or weird, and the exhausting process of monitoring her every word, expression, and movement for potential embarrassment. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I sit at my desk, trying to appear nonchalant, but my heart is racing like a jackrabbit on Red Bull. We're doing a group project on photosynthesis, and I've been paired with Alex, the star quarterback of the school football team. I've had a crush on him for what feels like forever, but every time I try to muster up the courage to talk to him, I freeze up like a deer in the headlights. The classroom is quiet, except for the projector squeaking away as our teacher flips through the slides. I fidget with my pencil, trying to calm my hands, but they're shaking like leaves. Why is my brain so loud? Why can't it just shut up for once? I try to tune it out, focusing on the task at hand, but my mind is a million miles away, replaying every embarrassing moment I've ever had around Alex. Okay, okay, stay calm, just be normal, just be normal. Don't overthink it. We're just working on a project, it's not a date. But what if he thinks I'm weird? What if I say something stupid? What if I trip over my own feet? Ugh, stop it, stop it, stop it. I take a deep breath, trying to center myself, but my eyes keep drifting to Alex, who's sitting across from me, oblivious to my inner turmoil. He's smiling at me, and I feel my face heat up like a toaster. Why is he smiling at me? Is he flirting with me? No, no, no, don't be ridiculous. He's just being friendly. Yeah, that's it. Friendly. I try to play it cool, but my voice is barely above a whisper. "Uh, so, what do you think about the whole light-dependent reaction thing?" I ask, trying to sound nonchalant. Alex looks at me, and I'm sure he can see right through me. "Yeah, I think it's pretty cool," he says, his eyes crinkling at the corners. "I mean, who knew plants could make their own food?" I try to respond, but my brain is a jumbled mess. Do I talk about the Krebs cycle? Or the Calvin cycle? Or something else entirely? I stare blankly at the slide, trying to gather my thoughts. The projector squeaks again, and I jump, my heart racing. "Uh, yeah, it's pretty fascinating," I say finally, trying to sound confident. "I mean, it's like, the plants are basically superheroes or something." Alex chuckles, and I feel my face grow hotter. Why is he laughing at me? Am I being funny? I'm not funny, I'm just being awkward. I try to laugh along, but it comes out as a awkward squeak. The rest of the class is a blur, with me stumbling over my words and Alex responding with easy, effortless answers. I feel like I'm watching myself from outside my body, like I'm a spectator at a weird, cringe-worthy comedy show. I'm mortified. As the class ends, Alex turns to me and says, "Hey, want to grab lunch together sometime? Maybe we can work on that project some more?" My heart skips a beat, and I feel like I've been punched in the gut. What does he mean? Is he asking me out? Oh my god, I think I'm going to die. I try to play it cool, but my voice is barely above a whisper. "Uh, yeah, that sounds great," I say, trying to sound nonchalant. "I mean, sure, that would be cool." As we pack up our things, I feel like I'm walking on eggshells, waiting for him to notice me, to see me for who I really am. But he just smiles and says, "See you later, Emma," and walks away, leaving me feeling like I've just been dropped into a black hole. I sit back down in my chair, feeling like I've been put on display like a specimen in a lab. My brain is racing, replaying every conversation, every word, every movement. What did I do wrong? What did I say? Why did I say that? I try to calm myself down, but my thoughts are a jumbled mess, like a tornado inside my head. The classroom is empty now, except for the projector, which is still squeaking away, reminding me of my own ineptitude. I take a deep breath, trying to quiet my brain, but it's no use. I'm stuck in this never-ending cycle of overthinking, analyzing, and self-doubt. As I pack up my things, I catch a glimpse of myself in the mirror, and I barely recognize the person staring back at me. I look like a nervous wreck, like I've been put through a wringer. But I take a deep breath, and I try to remind myself that it's okay to be awkward, to be me. Maybe, just maybe, Alex likes me for who I am, not for some idealized version of myself. Maybe he sees the real me, and likes it. Maybe I can be brave enough to be myself, even if that means being a little weird.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece attempts the assignment: an 8th-grade girl with social anxiety, internal monologue dominating, crush on a classmate during a science project. It hits the basic beats — rehearsed conversations, self-monitoring, fear of seeming weird — but in a generic, clichéd way. The voice reads more like an adult imitating a teen than an authentic intelligent 13-year-old; the narrator is described as articulate internally, yet the internal voice relies on stock similes ("jackrabbit on Red Bull," "deer in the headlights," "face heat up like a toaster"). The crush is a "star quarterback," a lazy trope that undercuts the "intelligent girl" framing and adds nothing specific. The dialogue is thin and functional; Alex is a cardboard nice-boy with no texture. The ending pivots abruptly to optimism ("Maybe he can be brave enough to be myself") without earning it — an unearned positive resolution that contradicts the established anxiety spiral. Some telling moments exist (the "superheroes" line, the squeaking projector as recurring anxiety trigger), but much of the emotion is stated rather than shown ("I'm mortified," "I feel like I've been punched in the gut"). The prose is competent but cluttered with mixed metaphors and repetitive reassurance loops. It's coherent and readable, but unsurprising throughout. + First-person unique perspective: Rehearsing Rejection — Score: 42.2
Prompt:
Rehearsing Rejection Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of someone with borderline personality disorder who frequently plays out worst-case scenarios of how friends and loved ones might judge them. They construct elaborate internal dialogues, complete with imagined criticisms and their own defensive responses. The narrative takes place as they prepare to meet a close friend & recent lover, who has been somewhat distant lately, triggering their abandonment fears. Their inner monologue should reveal how they cycle between anticipating rejection, mentally rehearsing confrontations, and developing preemptive resentments as protective measures. Incorporate the *actual* events & interactions that are *not* in fact judgemental or involving rejection (in fact, the opposite), and represent how these are interpreted, how the validation & love is desperately felt & needed -- and then immediately questioned. Capture the cognitive dissonance in this struggle. Include moments where they briefly recognize the pattern but struggle to interrupt it, showing limited (and selective) self awareness, competing with the compelling nature of these imagined scenarios. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Ugh, why did I agree to meet up with her tonight? I can already feel the knot in my stomach, the familiar dread creeping up my spine. My phone's battery is at 2%, and I'm stuck in this cramped, dimly lit coffee shop, surrounded by the faint hum of strangers' conversations. It's like the world is conspiring against me. What if she's not into me anymore? What if she's been talking to someone else behind my back? What if she's just going through the motions, pretending to care about me? The thoughts are a never-ending cycle, and I've been running through this script for hours, rehearsing every possible scenario. "Okay, so you walk in, and she's not smiling at you. She's not making eye contact. She's just sitting there, sipping her coffee, clearly not interested. You try to make small talk, but she's monosyllabic, like she's humoring you. You start to feel like you're not good enough, that you're just a placeholder until she gets back to her normal life." No, no, no, no, no. Don't go there. Don't even think that. She's just stressed, that's all. We've been distant lately, but that doesn't mean she doesn't care. (pauses) Wait, why am I defending myself like this? Why am I so convinced she's judging me? Ugh, I know I am. It's just my brain's way of trying to protect me. But it's like, why do I always fall for this? Why do I always assume the worst? (rolls eyes) I mean, it's not like I'm a fragile, broken egg or something. (She walks in, smiling, and my phone buzzes with a text from an unknown number. My heart skips a beat. "Hey, just got here. Running a bit late.") Oh, great. Now she's running late. That's just perfect. That's just what I needed. Another thing to make me feel like I'm not worth her time. (She arrives, looks around the coffee shop, spots me, and smiles warmly. She takes a seat next to me, and we exchange awkward hellos.) Ugh, why is she smiling at me like that? Is she up to something? Is she just being nice to be nice? (notices the warmth of her hand as she takes mine) Ow, stop it. Stop analyzing everything so much. Just relax. (She orders us a drink, and we chat about our weeks. She asks about my job, and I launch into a passionate speech about my latest project. She listens attentively, nodding and making supportive noises.) No, no, no, this can't be happening. This is just a setup. She's just pretending to care about me, waiting for the perfect moment to pounce and drop the hammer. (she laughs at one of my jokes) Oh, great. Now she's laughing at me. That's just it. She's laughing at me. (She reaches out and gently brushes a strand of hair behind my ear. My heart leaps, and I feel a rush of validation.) Ah, wait, what's this? Is she...is she... (pauses, feeling a mix of excitement and trepidation) No, no, no. Don't get ahead of yourself. She's just being friendly. That's all. (tries to calm down, but can't shake the feeling of being seen and loved) (She leans in, whispers "I've missed you so much" in my ear. I feel like I'm melting into her, like I'm home.) Oh, this is it. This is the moment when she's going to totally freak out and walk out the door. No, no, no. Don't believe it. This is just a ruse. (She takes my hand again, and we sit in comfortable silence for a moment, savoring the connection. My phone's battery is now at 1%.) Okay, okay. Maybe, just maybe, I was wrong. Maybe this is real. Maybe she's still into me. (pauses) But what if...what if I'm just reading too much into this? What if I'm just projecting my own fears and doubts onto her? (She breaks the silence, asks me to show her around the city. I get lost in the moment, laughing and joking, feeling like I'm walking on air.) Ugh, stop it. Stop getting all caught up in this. You're going to get hurt. You're going to get broken. (tries to pull away from her, but she holds me tighter) No, no, no. Don't push me away. Don't make me doubt it. Just...just hold onto this feeling. Just hold onto the hope that this might actually be real. (The evening wears on, and we linger in the coffee shop, our hands still entwined. My phone's battery dies, and she pulls out her own phone to charge it.) Shut. Up. Just shut. It. Off. (laughs, feeling a mix of relief and embarrassment) Great, now we're stuck here, stuck in this moment, with no way to escape. (She leans in, whispers "I'm glad we're doing this. I missed you too.") Wait, what? What's happening? Why am I feeling this way? (tries to rationalize, but can't shake the sense of wonder and excitement) No, no, no. Don't get ahead of yourself. This is just...just a fluke. A one-time thing. (pauses, looks into her eyes) But what if...what if this isn't a fluke? What if this is real? (She smiles, and I feel like I'm falling into the abyss, into the unknown. My phone, now charged and silenced, seems to hold a promise: all I need is trust, and I'll be set.)

+ Physical-spatial: Blood Debt — Score: 43.8
Prompt:
"Blood Debt" Write a vivid third-person narrative depicting an unsanctioned underground duel between two talented but hot-headed martial artists. Their schools have been embroiled in a generations-old rivalry, and despite strict prohibitions from their masters, these two younger fighters arrange a secret confrontation fueled by personal pride and simmering animosity. - **One fighter** represents **Jigen-ryū**, a traditional, aggressive style practiced historically by elite samurai clans. Its practitioners emphasize explosive offense, decisive strikes, and overwhelming speed. Known historically for their arrogance and disdain toward outsiders, practitioners consider their art superior and untouchable. - **The opposing fighter** employs a form of historical **Piguaquan**, a Chinese martial art traditionally practiced by commoners, bodyguards, and street fighters. This style emphasizes whipping, circular strikes, rapid footwork, unpredictable angles of attack, and evasive maneuvers. Practitioners often come from lower social classes and view the traditional elite as pretentious and detached from true combat effectiveness. The fight takes place in the crumbling ruins of an abandoned temple, now functioning as a hidden arena for illicit duels. Passionate supporters from both factions surround them, wagering money, pride, and reputation on the outcome. **Essential Instructions:** Maintain absolute physical coherence throughout—clearly track fighters' positions relative to each other and their environment. Precisely depict strikes, counters, momentum shifts, and spatial dynamics, allowing readers to clearly visualize the mechanics and choreography of the fight at every moment. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The air was heavy with tension as the two fighters, Kaito and Ryota, faced off in the abandoned temple's central courtyard. The crumbling stones beneath their feet seemed to whisper warnings of the chaos that was about to unfold. The only sound was the soft crackling of the lone torch, its flame dancing across the walls like a malevolent spirit. Kaito, a Jigen-ryū practitioner, sneered at Ryota, his eyes flashing with arrogance. His master, the renowned Sensei Akira, had warned him about this fight, the risks of succumbing to Ryota's Piguaquan style. Ryota, on the other hand, was a master of Piguaquan, a style that had long been scorned by the Jigen-ryū elite. He had been training in secret, fueled by a desire to prove that his art was not inferior, that it could rival the traditional styles of the samurai. His eyes gleamed with a fierce intensity, his movements economical and precise. The crowd, a mix of passionate supporters from both factions, held their breath as the duel began. The air was thick with tension, the only sound the soft crunch of gravel beneath the feet of the onlookers. The two fighters circled each other, sizing each other up, their movements a blur as they assessed their opponent's strengths and weaknesses. Kaito, ever the aggressor, charged forward, his fists flying in a flurry of rapid strikes. Ryota, anticipating the attack, sidestepped and counterattacked, his Piguaquan style allowing him to weave and dodge with ease. The two fighters clashed, their movements a whirlwind of steel and fists, the sound of their blows echoing off the temple walls. Kaito's Jigen-ryū training served him well, but Ryota's Piguaquan style proved to be more than a match for the samurai's brute force. The Piguaquan practitioner landed a series of lightning-fast jabs, each one finding its mark, and Kaito stumbled back, his eyes widening in surprise. Undeterred, Kaito regained his footing and launched himself at Ryota, his massive body hurtling toward his opponent like a force of nature. Ryota, anticipating the attack, sidestepped once more, using the momentum to send Kaito crashing into the stone wall behind him. The samurai stumbled back, his eyes blazing with anger and frustration. Ryota seized the opportunity, his Piguaquan style allowing him to exploit the opening. He landed a series of whipping strikes, his arms flailing like a snake, each blow biting deep into Kaito's defenses. The Jigen-ryū practitioner stumbled back, his movements slowing, his breathing growing labored. Ryota sensed victory within his grasp and pressed his advantage, landing a devastating combination of circular strikes that sent Kaito crashing to the ground. As Kaito struggled to rise, Ryota stood over him, his chest heaving with exertion, his eyes gleaming with triumph. The crowd erupted into cheers, their voices a deafening roar that threatened to consume the temple whole. Kaito's supporters wailed in despair, their faces twisted with anguish and disappointment. The torch, now dimmed by the intensity of the fight, flickered precariously, casting eerie shadows on the walls. The air was heavy with the scent of sweat and ozone, the very atmosphere charged with the electric tension of the duel. Ryota, his chest heaving, stood over his defeated opponent, his arm raised in victory. The crowd's cheers reached a fever pitch, their voices echoing off the temple walls as Ryota let out a triumphant cry. Kaito, his face twisted in a snarl of rage and humiliation, glared up at his opponent, a cold glint in his eye. In that moment, the centuries-old rivalry between the Jigen-ryū and Piguaquan factions seemed to reach a boiling point. The torch, now a burning ember, cast a golden glow over the temple, as if the very fabric of tradition was being rewritten in the heat of the moment. As the crowd's cheers died down, Ryota stood over Kaito, his chest heaving with exertion, his eyes gleaming with a fierce pride. The Piguaquan practitioner had proven himself a worthy adversary, and the Jigen-ryū school would never look at him the same way again. The rematch was inevitable, and the future of the martial arts was forever changed.

Momentum shifts are arbitrary—Kaito simply "stumbles back" repeatedly until he loses. The word count falls short of the requested 1000 words. There is no dialogue, so that criterion is excluded. Positives: the piece is coherent, easy to follow, and maintains a consistent if unremarkable tone throughout. It does not meander badly, and the ending's positivity is at least congruent with the outcome shown, if unearned in emotional weight. + Physical-spatial: Breaking Formation — Score: 40.0
Prompt:
**"Breaking Formation"** Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of a street dancer from a rough neighborhood who's won a scholarship to an elite K-pop training academy in Seoul. Desperately out of place culturally and technically, they struggle with a militaristically precise choreography that contradicts everything about their improvisational background. The punishing rehearsal takes place under a famous instructor known for breaking students to remake them, who sees potential in the narrator's raw talent but not their attitude. This scene MUST maintain complete physical coherence—each dance move, position shift, and spatial relationship must be tracked with precision throughout the studio. Paint an exceptionally vivid kinetic picture through descriptions that capture not just movement but the contrast between the narrator's fluid, expressive style and the mechanical precision demanded. Their internal monologue should reveal culture shock, class anxiety, and the threatening realization that success here might mean losing their authentic movement voice. Include technical terminology that highlights the collision of street and commercial styles, moments when muscle memory betrays them, and a crucial decision point about whether to conform or rebel. Model Output:
I stood at the edge of the studio, my eyes fixed on the rows of students in crisp, white uniforms, their bodies arranged in a neat, militaristic formation. The clock on the wall seemed to mock me, ticking away the precious seconds I had to prove myself. I was a fish out of water, a street dancer from the wrong side of the tracks, about to embark on a journey to the heart of K-pop hell. As I took my place among the others, I felt a familiar flutter in my chest, a mix of excitement and dread. I'd always been a free spirit, a dancer who lived for the moment, who let the music guide my movements. But here, in this sterile, precision-crafted environment, I was forced to conform to a different language, a different culture. The instructor, Sensei Kim, stood at the front of the room, her eyes blazing with an intensity that made my skin prickle. She was known for breaking students, for remaking them in her image, and I knew I was in for a long, hard road. "Formation, changi!" she barked, her voice like a whip. The students shifted, their bodies moving in perfect synchrony, like a well-oiled machine. I watched, mesmerized, as they executed the intricate footwork, their movements a blur of color and motion. But as I tried to follow, my body betrayed me. My feet stuttered, my arms flailed, and my hips wobbled. I felt like a rag doll, a puppet on strings, as I struggled to keep up. Sensei Kim's eyes narrowed, her gaze piercing. "No, no, no," she muttered, her voice dripping with disdain. "You're not listening. You're not feeling the music." I tried again, but my movements were clumsy, awkward. I felt like I was fighting a losing battle, like my body was rebelling against the strict, precise choreography. As the rehearsal wore on, I began to feel like I was losing myself, like I was being slowly drained of my creativity, my passion. I was a dancer, not a robot, and this was suffocating me. But Sensei Kim saw something in me, something that made her pause. She walked over to me, her eyes locked on mine, and I felt a jolt of electricity run through my body. "You have potential," she said, her voice low and husky. "But you need to learn to control it. To harness it." I felt a surge of frustration, of resistance. I didn't want to be controlled, I didn't want to be molded into some cookie-cutter K-pop idol. I wanted to be free, to dance like I wanted, not like I was told. But as I looked into Sensei Kim's eyes, I saw something there, something that made me pause. It was a glimmer of recognition, of understanding. She saw me, really saw me, and she knew that I was struggling. The clock on the wall ticked away, the seconds ticking by like hours. I knew I had to make a decision, to choose between conforming to the mold, or rebelling against it. I took a deep breath, feeling my body tense, my muscles coiled like a spring. I looked around the room, at the other students, at Sensei Kim, and I knew what I had to do. I dropped into a low, wide stance, my feet shoulder-width apart, my arms relaxed by my sides. I closed my eyes, feeling the music pulse through my body, and I let go. My body responded, moving in a fluid, improvisational dance that was both raw and beautiful. I felt like I was speaking my own language, like I was dancing from the heart. The room was silent, the only sound the ticking of the clock, the beat of my own music. I felt a rush of exhilaration, of freedom, as I let myself go, as I lost myself in the moment. Sensei Kim's eyes snapped open, her gaze fixed on me, and I knew I had taken a risk. I had taken a chance, and it was either going to pay off, or it was going to blow up in my face. But as I stood there, my body moving in perfect sync with the music, I knew that I had made the right choice. I had chosen to be true to myself, to dance like I wanted, not like I was told. The clock on the wall ticked away, the seconds counting down, but I didn't care. I was lost in the music, lost in the moment, and I knew that I would never be the same again. As the rehearsal came to an end, Sensei Kim approached me, a small smile playing on her lips. "You have potential," she said, her voice low and husky. "But you need to learn to harness it. To control it." I nodded, feeling a sense of determination, of resolve. I knew that I had a long road ahead of me, but I was ready. I was ready to learn, to grow, to become the dancer I was meant to be. But as I stood there, my body still moving to the music, I knew that I would never be just another K-pop idol. I would never be just another robot, dancing on autopilot. I would always be me, a dancer, a artist, a rebel. And with that, I knew that I was ready to take on the world.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response addresses the basic premise—a street dancer at an elite Seoul academy struggling against rigid choreography—but executes it with minimal craft. The physical coherence the prompt demands is largely absent: dance moves are described in vague, generic terms ("my feet stuttered, my arms flailed, my hips wobbled") with almost no spatial tracking, no technical terminology from either street or commercial dance vocabularies, and no concrete depiction of how bodies move through the studio. The "improvisational" climax is described only in abstractions ("a fluid, improvisational dance that was both raw and beautiful"). Cultural details are sloppy: "Sensei" is a Japanese honorific, jarring in a Korean setting, and "Formation, changi!" is meaningless. The instructor is a stock drill-sergeant archetype with no individuality. The narrative relies heavily on clichés ("fish out of water," "wrong side of the tracks," "cookie-cutter," "well-oiled machine") and tells rather than shows nearly every emotional beat ("I felt a surge of frustration, of resistance"). Structurally, the piece is repetitive—the clock motif recurs mechanically, Sensei Kim delivers the same "you have potential, harness it" speech twice nearly verbatim, and the narrator's internal conflict is restated rather than developed. The ending is incongruously triumphant: the narrator rebels, faces zero consequences, and receives warm approval, dissolving the tension the prompt built around the conform-or-rebel dilemma. The transformation is unearned—nothing is risked or lost. Prose is serviceable but flat, with short, choppy, declarative sentences and no rhythmic texture that might echo the dance subject matter. Emotionally, it gestures at class anxiety and culture shock without making them felt. + Gothic-fantasy-humour: Court of the Dreaming — Score: 41.3
Prompt:
Court of the Dreaming: Territorial Dream Dispute. Write a dark fantasy scene with comedic elements set in the Courts of Dream where a case is being heard to determine ownership of a contested dreamscape. Mora, the Slavic nightmare spirit who sits on sleepers' chests causing paralysis and terror, has recently invaded and subverted a carefully crafted recurring dream of a schoolboy, Max. The dream originally featured the boy lying in a field of wildflowers with his crush, always culminating in a muddled almost-kiss and a pleasant confusion of emotions. This dream was the meticulous work of "First Love" (a rosy-cheeked, slightly bashful entity who crafts formative romantic experiences). However, Mora has recently infiltrated the dreamscape, transforming the scene so that when the girl playfully mounts the boy, she morphs into a terrifying yet somehow still confusingly attractive vision who proceeds to sit on his chest and feed on his breath. Mora's defense largely consists of the practical argument that "a girl's gotta eat too, you know?" Write this scene as it unfolds in the courtroom of the Dreaming, with Dream's demeanour, typically impenetrable, betraying a sense of irritation at having to adjudicate such matters. The comedy should derive from the farcical situation, familiar elements of courtroom drama intersecting with the fantastical world of the Dreaming, and the droll portrayal of the characters and their interactions. The humor should be dark and understated throughout. Dream is tempted to some mischief in the resolution of the case. Use descriptives sparingly when setting scene. Write for readability & engagement. 1000 words.
Model Output:
The courtroom of the Dreaming was a labyrinth of twisted dreams, where the surreal and the bizarre reigned supreme. Dream, the ruler of this mystical realm, sat at the bench, its imposing figure shrouded in an aura of detachment. The air was heavy with the scent of forgotten terrors and the whispers of the subconscious. A spectral jury, comprised of wispy manifestations of forgotten fears and half-remembered anxieties, hovered near the ceiling, their ethereal forms quivering with anticipation. Before the judges' bench, a peculiar case was about to unfold. A schoolboy, Max, stood before the court, his eyes wide with worry, while Mora, the Slavic nightmare spirit, lounged against the wall, sipping a cup of dark, steaming liquid. A delicate, porcelain doll-like creature, First Love, perched on the edge of the defendant's table, her rosy cheeks flushed with indignation. "Order, please," Dream intoned, its voice like a forgotten requiem. "This court is now in session. The case before us today is Territorial Dream Dispute number 314-B. The plaintiff, Max, claims that the defendant, Mora, has invaded and subverted his carefully crafted dream. We will hear testimony from both sides." Mora, still chuckling to itself, took a sip from its cup, the sound sending a shiver down the spines of the spectral jury. First Love, meanwhile, folded its arms, its eyes flashing with a mix of hurt and embarrassment. "Your Honor, I didn't mean to do anything," Mora began, its voice like a rusty gate. "I just get hungry, you know? And dreams are like, so... abundant." It shrugged, its shoulders barely rising above the surface of the plush, crimson couch it occupied. Dream's expression turned from detached to exasperated. "Abundant? Abundant?! You've invaded a carefully crafted dream, subverted the emotional intimacy of a schoolboy, and fed on his fear and confusion. That's not abundant, Mora. That's just plain cruel." Mora snickered, the sound echoing through the courtroom like a cruel melody. "Hey, someone's gotta eat, right? And it might as well be me. You can't have all the fun, can you, Dream?" First Love's face reddened, its voice trembling. "That's not the point, Your Honor! The point is that this was my dream, and Mora has ruined it for Max. They were supposed to be in love, to share a tender moment. But now, it's all... twisted and... and... unrecognizable." The spectral jury shifted uncomfortably, their whispers swirling into a cacophony of discontent. Dream's expression remained impassive, but a glint of mischief danced in its eye. "Interesting, Mora," Dream said, its voice dripping with sarcasm. "You're arguing that a girl's gotta eat, just like everyone else. But I think we can do better than that. Let's see... How about this: if you want to eat, we can provide you with a nice, plump, nightmarescape to call your own. One that's all yours, with plenty of screaming, and terror, and confusion. Would you like that?" Mora's grin broadened, its eyes gleaming with excitement. "Oh, yeah! That sounds perfect, Your Honor. I'll take it." The courtroom erupted into chaos, the spectral jury quivering with laughter. First Love's face turned beet-red with rage, while Max looked as if he'd just been punched in the gut. Dream raised its hand, its voice booming through the chaos. "Order! Order, please! We will not be swayed by petty emotions. Mora, I hereby declare that you will return the dream to its original form. You will not feed on the fear and confusion of others without their consent." Mora pouted, but a hint of resignation crept into its voice. "Okay, fine. But just remember, someone's gotta eat around here." As the courtroom erupted into a cacophony of whispers and chuckles, Dream leaned forward, a mischievous glint in its eye. "And, Mora? Next time, try making a reservation. We wouldn't want any... accidents." The Slavic nightmare spirit cackled once more, its laughter weaving through the dreamscape like a dark, twisted melody, as the courtroom dissolved into chaos, and the case was dismissed.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response fulfills the basic premise — a courtroom scene in the Dreaming with Max, Mora, First Love, and an irritated Dream — but executes it poorly. The prose leans on stock phrasing ("voice like a forgotten requiem," "voice like a rusty gate," "dark, twisted melody"), which reads as generic dark-fantasy boilerplate rather than the droll, understated tone requested. The humor is broad and repetitive rather than understated: Mora says "someone's gotta eat" three times, and the jury "quivers" repeatedly. The plot logic collapses in the resolution — Dream first offers Mora her own nightmarescape (mischief), Mora accepts, then Dream inexplicably reverses and orders the dream restored, making the middle exchange pointless. The prompt's key comedic detail — the girl morphing into a terrifying yet confusingly attractive vision sitting on Max's chest — is never dramatized; we're only told the dream was "twisted." Max is a passive prop with no interiority, and First Love's bashfulness is absent, replaced by generic indignation. Dream's irritation is stated rather than shown ("Dream's expression turned from detached to exasperated"). Pronouns for Mora waver between "it" and implied femininity. Dialogue is functional but flat, with courtroom drama beats (order in the court, sarcasm) deployed without wit. The ending's "try making a reservation" gag is a mild bright spot. Overall: competent structure, weak craft, little of the requested dark understatement. + Fanfic-crossover: Blood Moon — Score: 36.6
Prompt:
Blood Moon Alucard & Yennefer Write a story where Alucard (Hellsing) encounters Yennefer (The Witcher) in medieval Europe. Yennefer's magical abilities intrigue the vampire, while she's both repulsed and fascinated by his monstrous nature. Their power dynamic shifts constantly as they circle each other, neither willing to show weakness first. **Alucard (Hellsing)** Alucard is an ancient, immensely powerful vampire who works for the Hellsing Organization. He can regenerate from almost any wound and shape-shift at will. He's sadistic, arrogant, and takes pleasure in toying with his enemies before killing them. Despite being a monster, he despises weak vampires who can't accept their nature. He wears a distinctive red coat, wide-brimmed hat, and orange sunglasses. He uses massive handguns loaded with silver bullets. He seeks worthy opponents who can challenge him and potentially end his immortal existence. Famous quote: "The bird of Hermes is my name, eating my wings to make me tame." **Yennefer of Vengerberg (The Witcher)** Yennefer is a powerful sorceress who magically altered her appearance to hide her congenital hunchback. She's ambitious, stubborn, and manipulative when necessary. She has violet eyes and smells of lilac and gooseberries. Despite her cold exterior, she desperately wants to reverse her infertility caused by magical transformation. She's centuries old but appears young. She's politically savvy and refuses to be underestimated. She has a sarcastic sense of humor and little patience for fools. Though she rarely shows it, she's capable of deep love and loyalty. Famous quote: "I'm not sweet and I'm not a maiden in distress in need of saving." Third person, present tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The air was heavy with the scent of damp earth and decay as Alucard strode through the deserted village, his long coat billowing behind him like a dark cloud. His eyes scanned the streets, searching for any sign of his quarry. He had been tracking a powerful sorceress for days, and his instincts told him she was close. As he turned a corner, a figure emerged from the shadows. She was a woman with piercing violet eyes and a lilac-scented aura that seemed to draw him in. Alucard's gaze narrowed as he took in her appearance, his mind racing with possibilities. The woman, Yennefer of Vengerberg, stood tall, her eyes flashing with a mixture of curiosity and wariness. She was no stranger to magic, and Alucard's vampiric nature was instantly apparent to her. Yet, she seemed intrigued by his monstrous form, her gaze lingering on the sharp fangs and piercing eyes that seemed to bore into her very soul. "Who are you?" Yennefer asked, her voice low and husky, as she sized Alucard up. "I am Alucard," he replied, his deep voice dripping with amusement. "And you, my dear, are a sorceress of considerable talent. Though I suspect you're not aware of just how considerable." Yennefer's eyes flashed with anger, but Alucard merely chuckled, his eyes glinting with amusement. He knew he had her right where he wanted her – on the defensive. "You're not like any vampire I've ever met," Yennefer said, her voice a little softer now. "Most of them are...weak. Cowardly. You, on the other hand, seem to take pleasure in being a monster." Alucard's smile grew wider, his fangs glinting in the moonlight. "Ah, but that's where you're wrong, my dear. I am the epitome of power. The greatest vampire the world has ever known. And I take pleasure in toying with my prey before I kill them. It's only sporting, don't you think?" Yennefer's eyes narrowed, her gaze never leaving Alucard's face. "I think you're a sadist," she spat, her voice venomous. Alucard's smile never wavered. "Perhaps," he said, his voice dripping with sarcasm. "But at least I'm not a monster who's been transformed against her will. You, on the other hand, seem to have a certain...fascination with the idea of being a sorceress. Don't you, my dear?" Yennefer's face twisted in a mixture of anger and shame, but Alucard merely chuckled, his eyes glinting with amusement. He knew he had her right where he wanted her – trapped in a web of her own desires and insecurities. The two of them circled each other, neither willing to make the first move. Alucard's massive handguns hung at his hips, loaded with silver bullets that would be effective against any supernatural creature. Yennefer, meanwhile, seemed to be drawing energy from the air around her, her eyes flashing with magical power. As they danced around each other, their power dynamic shifted constantly. Alucard was the predator, the one with the superior strength and speed. But Yennefer was no fool – she was a sorceress, and she knew how to use her magic to her advantage. The game was on, and neither of them was willing to back down. "I think we're going to get along just fine, Alucard," Yennefer said, her voice dripping with sarcasm. "I've never met a vampire as...entertaining as you." Alucard's smile grew wider, his fangs glinting in the moonlight. "I think you'll find that I'm a bit more...challenging than you're used to," he said, his voice dripping with menace. Yennefer's eyes flashed with anger, but Alucard merely chuckled, his eyes glinting with amusement. He knew he had her right where he wanted her – trapped in a web of her own desires and insecurities. And with that, the game was on.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece is a mediocre execution of a promising crossover premise. It adheres loosely to the prompt — both characters appear, they circle each other, the power dynamic is mentioned — but it fails on the key instruction of third person, present tense, instead written in past tense. It also falls well short of the 1000-word target, ending abruptly around 700 words with no real resolution or escalation. Character work is shallow. Alucard's dialogue is generic villain-speak ("my dear," "dripping with menace") with none of his distinctive wit, philosophy, or menace — no reference to his famous quote, his guns are mentioned but never used, and his shape-shifting and sadism are described rather than demonstrated. Yennefer fares slightly better in concept, but her dialogue is flat and she lacks her signature sarcasm and political sharpness. Her famous quote's spirit is entirely absent. The line about being "transformed against her will" gestures at her backstory but is muddled and inaccurate in phrasing. The prose is the biggest weakness. It is astonishingly repetitive: "dripping with sarcasm," "dripping with menace," "eyes glinting with amusement," "smile grew wider, fangs glinting in the moonlight" — entire sentences are duplicated verbatim ("He knew he had her right where he wanted her – trapped in a web of her own desires and insecurities" appears twice; "the game was on" appears twice). This reads like unedited filler generation. The narration is almost entirely tell-don't-show: we are told the power dynamic shifts, that she's intrigued and repulsed, that he's toying with her, without a single scene of actual action, magic, or violence demonstrating any of it. The story ends before anything happens — no confrontation, no payoff, just a repeated declaration that "the game was on." Dialogue is weak: expository, on-the-nose, and interchangeable between characters. There is no emotional complexity, no tension beyond stated assertions, and no imagery beyond clichés (coat billowing "like a dark cloud," moonlight glinting). The ending is not incongruously positive so much as nonexistent. + Fanfic-crossover: Prison Strings — Score: 39.8
Prompt:
Prison Strings Jolyne Cujoh & Makima Write a story where Jolyne Cujoh (JoJo's Bizarre Adventure) and Makima (Chainsaw Man) cross paths at a supernatural prison facility. Jolyne is immediately suspicious of Makima's falsely sweet demeanor, while Makima is intrigued by Jolyne's Stand ability. Their battle of wits escalates dangerously as Jolyne realizes how manipulative Makima truly is. **Jolyne Cujoh (JoJo's Bizarre Adventure)** Jolyne is the daughter of Jotaro Kujo and the first female JoJo protagonist. Her Stand, Stone Free, allows her to unravel her body into string that she can control. She's hot-headed, foul-mouthed, and doesn't take disrespect from anyone. She was framed for murder and sent to prison. She has a distinctive butterfly tattoo on her arm and wears her hair in two buns. Despite her tough exterior, she's deeply loyal to her friends and family. She can be impulsive but is highly adaptable in battle. She inherited her father's ability to stay cool under pressure but is more emotionally expressive. Famous quote: "If there's no Mickey, this shit ain't Disney!" **Makima (Chainsaw Man)** Makima appears as a young woman with auburn hair and ringed yellow eyes. She's the Control Devil who can dominate anyone she considers beneath her (nearly everyone). She maintains a pleasant, soft-spoken demeanor that masks her manipulative, cold nature. She can create contracts with humans and devils, often exploiting their desires. She's obsessed with Chainsaw Man and wants to control him. She keeps numerous people and devils as pets on leashes. She's practically immortal due to a contract with the Prime Minister. She views humans as lesser beings but mimics human emotions perfectly. Famous quote: "It's okay. Dogs are supposed to die for their owners." Third person, present tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
Jolyne Cujoh trudged down the dimly lit corridor, her eyes scanning the surroundings with a mix of suspicion and annoyance. She had been imprisoned in this supernatural prison facility for what felt like an eternity, and she was starting to lose her patience. Her Stone Free Stand allowed her to unravel her body into strings, making it difficult for anyone to get a good grip on her, but it also made her a sitting duck for anyone with a few tricks up their sleeve. As she turned a corner, she spotted a woman with auburnair and h ringed yellow eyes standing against the wall, observing her with an unnervingly calm demeanor. Jolyne's instincts immediately went on high alert. There was something off about this woman, something that made Jolyne's skin crawl. The woman, Makima, smiled sweetly as Jolyne approached. "Ah, a new arrival, I see," she said, her voice dripping with honey. "Welcome to our little home. I'm Makima, the director of this... establishment." Jolyne's eyes narrowed. "What kind of prison is this?" she spat, her hand instinctively going to the knife she wore on her belt. Makima chuckled, her eyes glinting with amusement. "Oh, it's a special one. A place where the most... interesting souls come to play. And you, my dear, are quite the interesting specimen." Jolyne snorted. "Save it, sweetheart. I know what kind of prison this is. You're not exactly subtle." Makima's smile never wavered. "t thAh, buat's where you're wrong, Jolyne Cujoh. I'm not subtle. I'm... refined. And I can see the potential in you. Your Stand, for instance. Stone Free, isn't it?" Jolyne's eyes flashed with anger, but Makima's words struck a chord. She knew that Makima was trying to gauge her, to figure out what made her tick. Jolyne's instincts screamed at her to be cautious, but her curiosity got the better of her. "What do you want, Makima?" Jolyne asked, her voice a little softer. Makima's eyes sparkled with interest. "Oh, I think we can help each other, Jolyne. You see, I have a... proposal for you. One that could get you out of this place in no time." Jolyne raised an eyebrow. "What kind of proposal?" Makima's smile grew wider. "Why don't we play a little game? A game of cat and mouse, if you will. If you can defeat my... pet, Denji, you free'll be to leave this place. But if you lose... well, let's just say you'll be staying here for a while longer." Jolyne's eyes narrowed. Denji, the Chainsaw Man, was a formidable opponent, even without his Devil powers. But she wasn't one to back down from a challenge. "Alright, I'll play your game," Jolyne said, her voice dripping with sarcasm. "But if I win, you have to let me go. No tricks, no strings attached." Makima's eyes gleamed with excitement. "Oh, I think we can make an exception to that rule, Jolyne. After all, I have a few... strings of my own to pull." The air seemed to thicken with tension as Jolyne and Makima sized each other up, their eyes locked in a battle of wits. Jolyne knew that this was going to be a fight to the death, and she was ready to take Makima down. But as she looked into those ringed yellow eyes, she realized that Makima was not the only one with tricks up her sleeve. The game was on, and Jolyne was ready to play. But little did she know, Makima had already begun to manipulate the strings, pulling the marionette to dance to her tune.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fails on several fundamental instruction points: it is written in past tense rather than the requested present tense, and it runs roughly 600 words against the 1000-word target. It also contains obvious text corruption ("auburnair and h ringed", "t thAh, buat's where you're wrong", "you free'll be to leave"), which severely damages readability and signals carelessness. Beyond mechanical failures, the story is a setup without payoff. The prompt asks for a battle of wits that "escalates dangerously" as Jolyne realizes Makima's manipulative nature; instead, the piece ends just as the game is proposed, with no escalation, no demonstration of Makima's control abilities, and no real use of Stone Free. Jolyne's characterization is thin — she's generically angry and suspicious, with none of her foul-mouthed wit or adaptability on display. Makima fares slightly better in that her honeyed menace is gestured at, but her dialogue is a string of stock villain tics ("my dear", ellipses, "strings of my own to pull") rather than her specific unsettling politeness. The prose is heavily clichéd: "voice dripping with honey", "eyes narrowed", "sized each other up", "tricks up her sleeve" (used twice), "battle of wits", "the game was on". There is little concrete imagery — the prison is "dimly lit" and nothing else. Dialogue is functional but flat, with Makima's lines indistinguishable from any generic manipulator. The narrative repeatedly tells us what characters feel ("Jolyne's instincts screamed at her to be cautious") rather than showing it. The ending's shift to "little did she know, Makima had already begun to manipulate the strings" is a clichéd omniscient flourish that deflates tension rather than building it, and the "strings" pun is groan-worthy. Positives: the premise is competently staged, the two characters meet plausibly, and the pacing of the opening is serviceable. But the piece reads as a first draft outline of a story rather than the story itself. + Scp: Notes from the Future — Score: 41.3
Prompt:
Write an SCP report about a researcher who begins finding handwritten notes from their future self, warning about a containment breach that hasn't happened yet. The notes become increasingly desperate and incoherent, sometimes containing information that contradicts known reality. Include the Foundation's attempts to determine if this is an actual temporal anomaly or psychological manipulation by a contained entity, and the researcher's struggle to trust their own perception. First person (researcher's perspective with interspersed notes), past tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
I've been working on Project EREBO, a research initiative to study anomalous phenomena in the realm of temporal dynamics. My team and I have been collecting data on various temporal anomalies, but nothing could have prepared me for what I've been experiencing. **Initial Notes - 23:47, 15/02/20** I've started finding notes in my office, seemingly written by myself. At first, I thought it was just my imagination or a prank by a colleague. But the handwriting is unmistakable - it's mine, but it's not. The paper is standard Foundation issue, and I'm certain I've never written these words before. The first message reads: "They're coming for us. Don't trust anyone. The anomaly is growing. It's not just a containment breach, it's a doorway to... to something else. Be careful." I've tried to brush it off as a hallucination, but the notes keep appearing. They're always on my desk, in my office, or in hidden corners of the facility. The handwriting is always the same, but the content is... different. Some of the messages are cryptic, while others are explicit. They seem to be warning me about events that haven't occurred yet. **Later that day** I've been experiencing some... visions, I suppose you'd call them. Fragmented images of the future, but they're not mine. I see glimpses of the anomaly, a swirling vortex of colors, and... things moving through it. The notes have become more frequent, and the language is changing. It's like they're trying to convey a sense of urgency, but I'm not sure what's real and what's just... not real. **February 17, 20** I've started to question my own perception of reality. Am I losing my mind? The notes seem to be coming from my future self, but what if it's not? What if someone, or something, is manipulating me? I've started to feel like I'm trapped in a never-ending loop, reliving the same events over and over. **February 20, 20** I've had a breakthrough. I think I understand what's happening. The notes are a warning, a desperate attempt to prevent the anomaly from breaching containment. But it's not just a warning - it's a message from my future self, trying to change the course of events. I've been trying to communicate with my past self, to prevent the anomaly from growing, but it's like trying to hold water in my hands. **February 22, 20** I've been experiencing... glitches. Moments where I see things out of the corner of my eye, only to turn around and find nothing. I'm starting to wonder if I'm going mad. The notes are becoming more frequent, more urgent. They're telling me to stop the experiment, to abandon the research. But I know I've made too much progress. **February 25, 20** I've been seeing things that can't be explained. I see glimpses of alternate realities, of parallel universes. The notes are telling me that the anomaly is a doorway to these other realities, and that we're not alone. I've been trying to communicate with... the others, but I'm not sure who they are or where they're from. **March 1, 20** I've lost count of how many notes I've found. They're everywhere, even in my dreams. I'm starting to feel like I'm trapped in a never-ending nightmare. The Foundation is starting to take notice, but they don't believe me. They think I'm experiencing some kind of... psychological breakdown. **March 5, 20** I've had a breakthrough. I think I understand the anomaly. It's not just a doorway to other realities - it's a doorway to our own past. The notes are telling me that I've been following a path that's doomed from the start. I've been trying to prevent the anomaly from breaching containment, but I've been going in circles. I've been trying to change the course of events, but I'm not sure who or what I'm trying to save. **March 10, 20** I've stopped trying to make sense of it all. I've stopped trying to write these notes. The Foundation has taken me in for evaluation, and I'm... I'm not sure what's real and what's not. I'm starting to wonder if I'm just a puppet, playing a role in a game I don't understand. The notes are still there, still whispering in my ear, but I've learned to ignore them. **March 15, 20** I've seen the anomaly. It's a swirling vortex of colors, pulsing with energy. I've seen things moving through it, things that defy explanation. I've seen... me, but not me. I've seen a version of myself that's... different. I don't know what's real, what's not. **Present day** I'm writing this in secret, hidden away from prying eyes. I'm not sure how much longer I can keep going. The notes are still there, still whispering in my ear, but I've learned to ignore them. I've learned to question everything. I'm not sure what's real, what's not. I'm not sure what's happening. **Addendum** I've been trying to communicate with my future self, to find out what's real and what's not. I've been leaving notes for myself, trying to piece together the puzzle. I've been searching for answers in the notes, but they're like a hall of mirrors. I'm trapped in a maze of reflections, and I don't know which way to turn. I'm not sure how much longer I can keep going. I'm not sure if I'm alive or dead. I'm not sure if I'm writing this or just... dreaming.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece attempts a first-person researcher's log with interspersed notes, but largely fails to deliver on the prompt's key requirements. It is not formatted as an SCP report — no item number, object class, containment procedures, or clinical framing. The Foundation's investigation into whether this is a temporal anomaly or psychological manipulation is reduced to a single throwaway line ("The Foundation is starting to take notice, but they don't believe me"), which is one of the prompt's central requirements. The researcher's struggle to trust their perception is present but handled repetitively. The notes themselves — the second key requirement — are barely included. Only one note is quoted in full; the rest are summarized ("The notes are telling me..."), which violates the instruction to intersperse notes and exemplifies tell-don't-show. The escalating desperation and incoherence of the notes, plus contradictions with known reality, are asserted rather than demonstrated. Structurally, the piece is a series of diary entries that repeat the same beats: notes appear, reality is questioned, a "breakthrough" occurs (twice, with no meaningful difference), confusion deepens. The word "breakthrough" is used twice for the same non-event. The prose is flat and repetitive — "I'm not sure what's real and what's not" appears verbatim multiple times, ending paragraphs in near-identical fashion. There is no escalation of stakes, no concrete detail about the anomaly, the facility, colleagues, or the containment breach itself. "Project EREBO" and "a swirling vortex of colors" are the extent of worldbuilding. The ending is an incoherent fizzle rather than a purposeful ambiguity. The addendum restates earlier material almost word-for-word. There are no characters besides the narrator, no dialogue, no distinct voice — the narrator has no personality, history, or specificity that would make the psychological unraveling affecting. The piece reads as a competent but hollow imitation of the requested format, likely because it pattern-matches "confused diary about anomalies" without understanding SCP conventions (clinical framing, redactions, addenda, institutional voice). Positives: the premise is inherently engaging, the chronological log structure is easy to follow, and a few moments (notes appearing "even in my dreams," the doubled self seen in the vortex) gesture toward genuine unease. But execution is monotonous, vague, and fails most explicit instructions. + Scp: Unmarked Doors Archive — Score: 38.4
Prompt:
Write an SCP entry about a series of identical unmarked doors appearing in abandoned buildings worldwide that lead to a massive, architecturally impossible archive. Documents within reference a previous global civilization destroyed by an unspecified containment failure. Foundation researchers discover references to their own organization under a different name, including personnel files for current staff dated centuries ago. Include exploration logs revealing sections of the archive actively being removed by entities resembling hazmat teams who flee when approached, and evidence that O5 Council members have visited the archive without authorization. Third person (exploration team perspective) with recovered document excerpts. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
**SCP-XXXX: "The Archive"** **Object Class:** Euclid **Special Containment Procedures:** SCP-XXXX is to be approached with caution. Any attempt to open or explore the archive without proper authorization is strictly forbidden. All unmarked doors leading to SCP-XXXX are to be sealed and tagged with "Do Not Enter" signs. Foundation personnel are prohibited from accessing SCP-XXXX without Level 3 clearance and explicit permission from at least two Level 4 personnel. Any breach of these protocols will result in immediate revocation of clearance and potential reclassification to Keter. **Description:** SCP-XXXX refers to a series of identical, unmarked doors appearing in abandoned buildings worldwide. These doors are identical in appearance, with scratches around the doorknob area that appear to be some form of lock or mechanism. Each door is approximately 1.5 meters tall and 0.8 meters wide. The doors are made of a durable material, resistant to weathering and decay. Upon entering the archive through one of these doors, researchers have reported encountering a vast, labyrinthine structure that appears to defy all laws of physics and architecture. The archive contains an enormous collection of documents, files, and artifacts, including papers, books, and relics from a previous global civilization. The contents of the archive are highly organized and seem to be cataloged with precision. However, the archive also contains disturbing references to the Foundation's own history. Researchers have discovered personnel files for current Foundation staff, dated centuries ago, detailing their roles and responsibilities. Additionally, documents within the archive mention the "Organization of the New Dawn" (OND), a predecessor organization to the Foundation. These documents imply that the OND was responsible for containing and studying anomalous entities, but were ultimately destroyed due to a catastrophic containment failure. Exploration logs from researchers who have ventured into the archive reveal that sections of the structure are actively being removed by entities resembling hazmat teams. These teams are equipped with advanced equipment and seem to be working with precision to extract specific items from the archive. However, when approached or confronted, these teams immediately flee, often leaving behind evidence of their presence, including gloves, hazmat suits, and other personal items. Further investigation has revealed that several O5 Council members have visited the archive without authorization. These visits were often unannounced and took place in the dead of night, with the Council members reportedly taking great care to avoid detection. **Recovered Document Excerpts:** * **Personal File:** "Dr. Elara Vex, Researcher, OND" (dated 1923) * "Project: Erebus" - "Objective: Containment and study of anomalous entity 'Erebus.' Results: catastrophic failure. Entity escaped containment, resulting in global devastation." * "Personal Notes" - "I fear that we have made a terrible mistake. The entity is not what we thought it was. It is not just a creature, but a force that threatens to destroy everything we hold dear." * **Memorandum:** "O5 Council Meeting, 1955" (unsigned) * "Subject: Containment Failure and Global Consequences" - "We have been made aware of a catastrophic containment failure that has resulted in the destruction of a global civilization. We are working to establish a new organization to contain and study anomalous entities." * "Recommendations" - "Establish a new organization, separate from the OND, to focus on containment and research. Allocate resources and personnel to support this effort." * **Artifact:** "Foundation Charter" (dated 1975) * "Article 1: Purpose" - "The Foundation shall be dedicated to the study, containment, and research of anomalous entities and phenomena. The Foundation shall work to protect humanity from threats both internal and external." * "Article 3: Structure" - "The Foundation shall be governed by the O5 Council, composed of five senior members who shall oversee the organization's operations and make key decisions."
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece attempts an SCP format but reads more like a summary of the requested prompt than an actual executed creative work. It hits the bullet points—doors, archive, prior civilization, OND predecessor, hazmat entities fleeing, unauthorized O5 visits—but does so in flat, declarative prose with almost no atmospheric detail. Most critically, it omits the exploration logs the prompt explicitly requested: instead of immersive third-person exploration logs, we get a single description paragraph stating that "exploration logs reveal" the hazmat teams. This is a significant failure of adherence and of the show-don't-write principle. The recovered document excerpts are the strongest section but suffer from thin, generic content. The dates create internal confusion: personnel files for current staff "dated centuries ago" was the prompt's requirement, but the excerpt is dated 1923 and refers to a researcher with no apparent link to present-day staff. The 1955 memorandum casually announcing the destruction of a global civilization lacks the weight and redaction-style clinical texture authentic SCP documents carry. The "Foundation Charter" dated 1975 contradicts real Foundation lore and the prompt's "centuries ago" framing, and naming the predecessor "Organization of the New Dawn" is generic. Prose is functional but mechanical; sentences are short, repetitive, and lacking in imagery. No dialogue to assess. No character perspective or voice, since it's purely summary-style description. Emotionally inert. The structure technically follows SCP conventions but without clinical precision, redactions, or the escalating-dread pacing that makes the format work. The hazmat-team entities fleeing when approached is conveyed in one bland sentence rather than dramatized. O5 unauthorized visits are mentioned then abandoned—no follow-through, no tension. Coherence is passable, but the piece is underdeveloped, likely under the 1000-word target, and wastes its genuinely strong premise. + Classic sci-fi: Offworlder (Le Guin) — Score: 40.7
Prompt:
Classic sci-fi: Offworlder (Author style: Le Guin) Write a chapter following a young diplomatic envoy sent to address tensions on a distant human colony where humanity has diverged into distinct subspecies through both natural adaptation and separation of lineages. Against protocol, the envoy slips away from official functions to walk the streets alone, encountering markets, ordinary citizens, and social structures that contradict the official briefings. Upon returning to their quarters, the envoy is reprimanded by Command, and is tasked with approaching the stationed Minister of Science (a political posting rather than scientific). A challenging conversation ensues, in which the envoy's wits and will are tested, by a seasoned political operator looking to test the mettle of the offworlder. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
Chapter 7: The Glyphs of Arkeia I walked the streets of Arkeia, my footsteps echoing off the towering stone buildings. The city was alive, a tapestry of sounds and smells that seemed to pulse with a rhythm all its own. I had been warned about the city's informality, its disregard for the formalities of the colony's governance. But I had to see it for myself, to experience the heartbeat of this place. As a diplomatic envoy, I had been schooled in the protocols of intercolonial relations. I knew the standard speeches, the carefully crafted responses to the most common questions. But I also knew that those protocols were designed to keep us separate, to maintain the artificial boundaries between our subspecies. The Zha'thik, with their angular features and chitinous exoskeletons, and the N'Tari, with their slender limbs and iridescent skin. We were all human, yet we were not the same. I turned a corner, and the scent of fresh bread wafted through the air. I followed the aroma to a bustling market, where vendors hawked their wares to a crowd of curious onlookers. I watched, entranced, as a young N'Tari girl expertly juggled a trio of glowing orbs, her eyes flashing with a light that seemed almost otherworldly. Nearby, a group of Zha'thik traders haggled over the price of a shipment of rare, crystalline minerals. I felt a pang of envy, wondering what it would be like to be able to speak their language, to understand the intricate web of customs and traditions that governed their lives. As I wandered through the market, I began to notice the subtle differences in the architecture. The buildings were adorned with intricate carvings, glyphs that seemed to reference old Earth myths. I recognized the image of the Great Mother, her curves and lines echoing the symbols of the ancient Earth cultures. But these glyphs were different, adapted to the unique biology of each subspecies. The Zha'thik had their own version of the myth, with its emphasis on strength and resilience. The N'Tari, meanwhile, had incorporated elements of the old myths into their own, often using them to describe the intricate patterns of their own skin. I felt a shiver run down my spine as I realized the implications of these adaptations. We were not just different species; we were different cultures, with our own histories and traditions. And yet, the official briefings had given me a sanitized view of the colony, a view that ignored the complexities and nuances of life here. I slipped back into the shadows, feeling a sense of disquiet. I had been warned about the dangers of getting too close to the locals, of letting my own biases and assumptions cloud my judgment. But I couldn't help myself. I had to see more, to experience the city in all its messy, beautiful complexity. As I made my way back to my quarters, I felt a sense of trepidation. I knew that I had been reprimanded by Command, that I had been tasked with reporting back to the Minister of Science. I had no idea what to expect, but I knew that I would have to be careful. The Minister was not a man to be trifled with, a seasoned politician with a reputation for ruthlessness. I pushed open the door to my quarters, and was greeted by the stern face of my commanding officer. "Envoy, we've received your report," she said, her voice cold and detached. "But it's clear that you've been getting too close to the locals. You need to focus on your mission, not indulge in frivolous exploration." I nodded, trying to keep my temper in check. "Yes, ma'am. I understand." But as I turned to leave, I heard a voice behind me. "Actually, Envoy, I think you're being too cautious. You need to get out there and experience the city for yourself. And I think I can help you do just that." I turned to see the Minister of Science standing in the doorway, a sly smile spreading across his face. "I've been expecting you," he said, his eyes glinting with amusement. "And I think we have a lot to discuss." I felt a shiver run down my spine as I realized the implications of his words. This was not going to be a straightforward conversation, not by any means. But I was ready, my wits and my will sharpened by my experiences on the streets of Arkeia. Bring it on, I thought, a sense of anticipation building in my chest.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response follows the prompt's broad beats (envoy slips away, market encounter, reprimand, Minister setup) but fails to deliver on the most important element: the challenging conversation with the Minister of Science never actually occurs. The chapter ends just as it begins, with the envoy thinking "Bring it on" — a cliffhanger rather than a scene. The reprimand is also compressed into a single flat exchange, and the Minister conveniently appears in the envoy's own quarters, which is both logistically odd and dramatically lazy. The worldbuilding is thin and generic. The subspecies names (Zha'thik, N'Tari) are stock sci-fi fantasy syllable-salad, and their descriptions ("angular features and chitinous exoskeletons," "iridescent skin") are surface-level. The Le Guin influence is entirely absent — no anthropological patience, no philosophical weight, no quiet observational intelligence. The glyphs referencing "old Earth myths" gesture at Le Guin's interest in myth but do nothing with it. The prose is repetitive ("I felt a shiver run down my spine" appears twice verbatim), heavy on filtering ("I watched," "I felt," "I realized"), and full of vague abstractions ("messy, beautiful complexity," "the heartbeat of this place"). Dialogue is minimal and stiff, and the commanding officer's reprimand is undercut by the Minister immediately contradicting her, which reads as incoherent rather than ironic. The narrator claims to have been "sharpened" by the street experience, but nothing shown on the streets would sharpen anything — this is an unearned transformation. The ending's "sense of anticipation" is incongruously upbeat given the disquiet established earlier. + Biopunk: Polymerase Chain Reaction — Score: 38.4
Prompt:
Biopunk -- Polymerase Chain Reaction Write a chapter set in a near-future post-apocalyptic world where corporate genetic engineering was designed with built-in obsolescence—engineered DNA lasting only one generation to maximize profits before societal collapse. Follow a biohacker working with salvaged equipment to splice viable food crops for their settlement using rapidly degrading genetic material. As world building / window dressing, include details about jury-rigged lab equipment, and the physical toll (and unexpected upsides) of using untested genetic modifications as medicine. The plot of this chapter is centred around a black market contract. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
Chapter 7: The Black Market Bargain I adjusted the makeshift spectrophotometer, a repurposed smartphone screen held aloft by a bent coat hanger, to compensate for the wobbly solar panel rig's unstable voltage. The lab equipment creaked and groaned around me, a testament to the resourcefulness of our settlement's DIY ethos. We'd salvaged this equipment from the ruins of a pre-apocalyptic world, jury-rigging it to suit our needs. It wasn't perfect, but it got the job done. As a biohacker, I'd learned to work with the limitations of our situation. We'd developed a system to engineer viable food crops using rapidly degrading genetic material, but it came with its own set of challenges. The DNA was only stable for a single generation, forcing us to constantly adapt and evolve our methods. It was a constant cat-and-mouse game with entropy, but it was one we'd grown accustomed to. The door to the lab creaked open, and a hooded figure slipped in, their face obscured by the shadows. They moved with a quiet confidence, a practiced air of stealth that belied their uncertain eyes. "Kael," I said, nodding at the stranger. "I didn't expect you to show up so soon." The figure pushed back its hood, revealing a young woman with a scar above her left eyebrow and a look of determination etched on her face. "I heard you were the best in the business," she said, her voice low and husky. "I need something specific." I raised an eyebrow, intrigued. "What kind of project?" The woman hesitated, glancing around the lab with a mixture of caution and curiosity. "I'm looking for a genetic modification that can...accelerate healing. I've got a client who's willing to pay top dollar for something that can help them recover from...injuries." I nodded, my mind already racing with possibilities. "I think I can help you with that. But it's not going to be cheap." The woman nodded, her eyes locked on mine. "I'm willing to pay top dollar. I've got a contract from a...reputable client. I just need something that works." I leaned back in my chair, steepling my fingers together as I considered the proposal. "I can offer you a genetic modification that can enhance healing factors, but it's going to require some...creative problem-solving." The woman's eyes narrowed, her expression a mixture of excitement and trepidation. "I'm listening." I gestured to a nearby workbench, where a selection of vials and test tubes held the promise of life. "We'll need to use some of the more...untested genetic material. The kind that's been left behind by the old world's genetic engineering." The woman's eyes widened as she took in the array of specimens, some of which looked like they'd been ripped straight from a horror movie. "You mean, like...the 'gastro-resistant' gene?" I nodded, a wry smile spreading across my face. "The same. It's not stable, but it's got potential. We can use it to create a healing factor that's...unpredictable, but effective." The woman's eyes locked on mine, a spark of understanding igniting between us. "I like the sound of that. How much will it cost?" I named a price that would make even the most seasoned biohacker blanch, but the woman didn't flinch. She nodded, a look of determination etched on her face. "I'll take it," she said, her voice firm. I nodded, a sense of excitement building in my chest. This was going to be a challenge, but I was up for it. I just hoped that the unknown variables wouldn't prove to be my downfall. As we began the process of splicing the genetic material, I couldn't help but feel a sense of trepidation. We were playing with fire here, tampering with the fundamental building blocks of life. But the payoff could be worth it – if we were lucky, and if we were smart. The woman worked alongside me, her hands moving with a precision that belied her uncertainty. Together, we coaxed the genetic material into shape, our fingers dancing across the workbench as we wove a new narrative into the fabric of life. It was a delicate balancing act, one that required finesse and luck. But as we worked, I couldn't shake the feeling that we were onto something big – something that could change the course of our lives, and the lives of those around us. The black market contract was a risk, but it was one we'd have to take. The world outside our settlement was treacherous, full of predators and opportunists. But inside, we had each other – and the promise of a better future. As we worked, the lab equipment creaked and groaned around us, a testament to the ingenuity and desperation that had brought us to this point. We were biohackers, working on the fringes of society, using the latest technology to create a new world – one that was fragile, and uncertain, but full of possibility. And as the genetic material began to take shape, I couldn't help but feel a sense of pride – pride in our ingenuity, our determination, and our refusal to accept the status quo. We were the outliers, the rebels, the ones who refused to play by the rules. And as we worked, I knew that we were creating something truly special – something that could change the course of history.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece hits the basic prompt beats superficially: a biohacker, salvaged equipment, a black market contract, and degrading genetic material are all mentioned. However, the execution is thin. The world-building is mostly asserted rather than shown — the "makeshift spectrophotometer" made from a smartphone screen and coat hanger is the only concrete jury-rigged detail, and it's physically dubious. The prompt's central premise (splicing viable food crops) is mentioned in passing but abandoned; the chapter pivots entirely to a healing-factor contract, and the food-crop work never matters. The "physical toll and unexpected upsides" of untested genetic medicine is entirely absent — a major instruction miss. The plot is minimal: a hooded stranger arrives, asks for a healing mod, negotiates a price, and they splice together with vague "fingers dancing across the workbench" hand-waving. There's no actual science-flavored process, no tension, no complication, no payoff. The "gastro-resistant gene" as a healing factor is nonsense even by soft-sci-fi standards. Prose problems are pervasive: heavy reliance on ellipses in dialogue ("a genetic modification that can...accelerate healing"), clichés ("cat-and-mouse game with entropy," "playing with fire," "weaved a new narrative into the fabric of life"), and repetitive constructions ("a testament to" appears twice; "a look of determination etched on her face" appears twice nearly verbatim). The ending swells into generic inspirational filler ("We were the outliers, the rebels...") that feels unearned given nothing was risked or resolved. Dialogue is functional but stilted, with both characters speaking in the same voice. Characters are stock: the cool confident biohacker, the husky-voiced mysterious woman. Emotion is told, not shown, throughout. Coherence is fine and the voice is consistent, but consistently mediocre. The piece reads like competent filler with no distinctive imagery, no stakes, and no payoff.
